# Gen 4 ideas (55 from 11 lenses)

The founder's contender is in FOUNDER_REFRAME.md (id FOUNDER).

## I01: TYPED THROUGH: a Deaf caller's typed words reach every Rutgers phone, carried by Verizon's network (lens accessibility, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services to Enhance Customer Experience, with a Pillar 1 element (an AI agent that answers RTT natively), self-score 65)

**One-liner:** Rutgers moved its staff phones to Microsoft Teams, and Microsoft's own documentation (updated Jul 9 2026) says Teams real-time text is 'not supported' on PSTN calls. So when a Deaf, hard-of-hearing, speech-disabled or phone-anxious student types a native RTT call from a Verizon phone to a Rutgers office, the words probably go nowhere. We measure that across about 150 published Rutgers numbers. Then we build a Verizon network-side RTT bridge on Verizon's Teams calling (Operator Connect, Direct Routing, Verizon Mobile for Teams). The office hears the typed words as speech, the caller sees the reply as live text, and the network vouches for who is calling.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED: (1) Microsoft Learn, 'Manage real-time text (RTT) in Teams' (updated_at 2026-07-09). The 'Not supported' list includes 'PSTN calls' and 'Emergency calling'. RTT works only in Teams meetings, group calls and 1:1 VoIP calls. https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/microsoftteams/teams-real-time-text (2) Rutgers OIT made Teams Phone a voice option in 2026 (page created 2026-02-04). Moving a user to Teams leaves the old desk phone able to call only 911. https://it.rutgers.edu/voice/microsoft-teams-phone/ (3) Rutgers' main numbers 848-445 and 848-932 belong to PAETEC ITEL/Windstream (OCN 829J), not Verizon. https://www.localcallingguide.com/lca_prefix.php?npa=848&nxx=445 (4) Title II has required this since 2010. 28 CFR 35.161(b): an automated-attendant or IVR system 'must provide effective real-time communication with individuals using auxiliary aids and services, including TTYs and all forms of FCC-approved telecommunications relay systems'. 35.161(c): a public entity 'shall respond to telephone calls from a telecommunications relay service ... in the same manner that it responds to other telephone calls.' https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-28/chapter-I/part-35/subpart-E/section-35.161 (5) The FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083 (released Oct 7 2026) finds gaps in 'customer service, AI-enabled communications'. It cites 'customer service channels that are not compatible with assistive technologies' and RDAs over 'recurrent problems where carriers refused to accept Video Relay Service (VRS) relay calls from deaf users'. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1083A1.pdf (6) The FCC consumer guide lists who RTT users can call: other RTT users, 911, 711 and TTY users. Voice-only lines are not on the list. Source: search-result text only, because the page returned 403 to our fetcher. https://www.fcc.gov/consumers/guides/real-time-text-improving-accessible-telecommunications (7) Rutgers ODS publishes a voice number (848.202.3111) and a fax on 732.445.3388 (a Verizon Piscataway 5ESS NXX). It lists no TTY or videophone number (fetched 2026-10-21). https://ods.rutgers.edu/ UNVERIFIED: what a voice-only line actually hears when a Verizon RTT call arrives (silence, Baudot tones or nothing). That is our day-1 experiment.
- **Verizon tech:** VoLTE/IMS Real-Time Text (3GPP MMTel T.140 / RFC 4103), native in the iPhone and Android dialers on Verizon. IMS-asserted caller identity plus STIR/SHAKEN full attestation, so the bridge can tell the callee 'this is the verified Verizon line ending 1234'. Verizon's Teams calling products: Operator Connect, Direct Routing and Verizon Mobile for Microsoft Teams (verified product pages: https://www.verizon.com/business/products/voice-collaboration/microsoft-teams/operator-connect and https://www.verizon.com/business/products/voice-collaboration/collaboration-platforms/microsoft-teams-for-collaboration/). In production the bridge is an IMS application server or media function on the Verizon side of the Teams SBC: it turns RTT into TTS toward the voice-only party, and the party's speech into ASR text sent back as RTT. Second deployment: Verizon's own care line, where an AI agent converses natively over RTT and authenticates the caller from the network identity instead of a PIN read aloud through an interpreter.
- **Verizon money:** Primary: E&PS calling revenue at Title II public entities. Every state and local government and every public university owes 35.161 compliance, and Teams Phone cannot carry PSTN RTT today. 'The Teams calling that a Deaf caller can actually reach' is a concrete differentiator for Verizon Operator Connect seats. Rutgers is a live win-back target: its PSTN numbers have sat with PAETEC/Windstream since about 2010. E&PS revenue was $13.5B in FY2025, down 4.8%. Formula with labeled assumptions: seats won x per-seat calling price (UNVERIFIED; Verizon publishes no Operator Connect price) x 12. Secondary: Verizon care. The FCC's Oct 7 2026 report records carriers refusing relay calls. An RTT-native AI agent with network authentication removes the third-party-verification refusal and the relay overhead on handle time (that overhead is UNVERIFIED). Schulman names service-call friction as a churn cause, and 1 bp of churn is about 90,000 net adds (Jan 30 2026 transcript: https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-02/vz_4q25_transcript_013026r.pdf). Honest size: modest. This is E&PS growth plus regulatory and brand protection, not a P&L line on its own.
- **Dated anchor:** Microsoft Teams RTT documentation updated 2026-07-09: PSTN calls and emergency calling are 'not supported'. Rutgers Teams Phone rollout (page created 2026-02-04). FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083, released Oct 7 2026, which names customer service, AI-enabled communications and carriers refusing VRS calls as gaps. FCC 26-56 (effective Oct 13 2026) removed the TTY-based TRS ASCII requirement, a sign that TTY is fading and RTT is the path. DOJ moved the Title II web/app deadline to Apr 26 2027, which pulls Rutgers' accessibility attention to the web while the phone channel, governed by 35.161 since 2010, goes unaudited.
- **Primary data:** (1) RTT and relay reachability audit of about 150 published Rutgers numbers: ODS, CAPS, Student Health, Financial Aid, Registrar, Housing, DoTS dispatch, department offices, and RUPD non-emergency only after coordinating with RUPD. For each number we record: does typed text arrive; what the callee hears; and, for a typed caller using an on-device type-to-speak tool, the minutes needed to get through the Teams auto-attendant to a human, compared with a voice caller. (2) From OIT: how many Rutgers numbers are on Teams vs Cisco vs analog/TTY, and which IVRs exist. (3) Short interviews with Deaf and hard-of-hearing students through ODS (consented, voluntary) about how they reach university offices today. (4) The same typed-call test against Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile care lines, as a carrier benchmark that only a student team can publish neutrally.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT (20-25 s): A student types 'I need to move my accommodated exam' into the iPhone's native RTT call to a Rutgers office. Cut to the office: a Teams phone rings, a staffer says 'Hello? ... Hello?' and hangs up. Caption: Microsoft, Jul 2026: RTT 'not supported' on PSTN calls. On-screen stat from our audit: 'N of 150 Rutgers numbers cannot receive a typed call.' Then the same call with TYPED THROUGH: the staffer hears a clear voice ('Verified caller, Verizon line ending 4471, typing: I need to move my exam'), replies, and the reply streams onto her phone as live text. POSTER TABLE: a live rig. A visitor's phone, or ours, places an RTT call to our bridge number. The bridge prototype is an Android default-dialer app using Android's RttCall API to read and write the RTT stream, a laptop running ASR/TTS, and a Twilio voice leg to a desk phone. Beside it, the audit heat map of Rutgers offices and a three-carrier care-line benchmark table.
- **Why unique:** LLM-default teams do not know RTT exists as a native carrier feature, do not know Microsoft's July 2026 doc excludes PSTN, and do not know Rutgers moved to Teams in 2026. The primary data (which Rutgers offices a typed call reaches) does not exist anywhere and costs one Verizon phone and a week. It is network-native: the fix lives where Verizon's IMS meets Teams calling, a place no app can reach on the institution's side. It also differs from our explored Lifeline Video (sign-language video QoS) and Silent 911 (texts to 911).
- **Biggest risk:** On-device tools (iOS Live Speech plus Live Captions, Pixel Call Assist) already let a Deaf iPhone or Pixel user type-to-speak on an ordinary voice call. A judge may say 'the phone already does this'. We must show the audit failures happen anyway: IVR timeouts, staff hanging up, no identity. The bridge lives in Verizon's IMS, so students can only emulate it off-network, which weakens Content. Verizon's Operator Connect price and Microsoft's plans for PSTN RTT are unknown, so Microsoft could close the gap. It could also read as a critique of Rutgers OIT in front of the CIO judge, so we frame it as a joint OIT pilot.

## I02: CAPTIONS BEFORE THE RADIO: the network captions your call before your bad signal garbles it (lens accessibility, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services to Enhance Customer Experience (AI at the network edge), self-score 63)

**One-liner:** Hard-of-hearing people now read their calls through on-device live captions. Those captions transcribe audio that has already been damaged by the user's weak indoor signal. Verizon's IMS holds the clean far-end audio before it crosses the radio. It can run ASR there and send the words down the call's own RTT channel, a few hundred bits per second that survive where voice breaks up. The user keeps their own number and dialer, with no caption phone and no second number. If Verizon or a partner is a certified ASR-only IP CTS provider, the TRS Fund pays $0.95 a minute.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED: (1) FCC DA 26-646 (Jun 30 2026) sets 2026-27 IP CTS compensation at '$0.95 for service using only automatic speech technology, $1.45 for service using a communications assistant'. The net TRS funding requirement is '$1,563,489,843, an increase of 73 million', and the internet-based TRS contribution factor is 0.02276 on carriers' end-user revenues, so Verizon already pays in. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-646A1.pdf (2) FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083 (Oct 7 2026): 'automated captioning systems may produce inaccurate captions, particularly for individuals with speech disabilities or speakers with accents'. RDAs cite 'call forwarding failures that blocked access to captioning'. Consumer groups say Deaf and hard-of-hearing users juggle 'at least two different telephone numbers'. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1083A1.pdf (3) 47 CFR 64.611 shows the two-number structure: a relay provider either ports the user's number or 'assign[s] that user a ... telephone number'. https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-F/section-64.611 (4) NJ DDHH's Equipment Distribution Program now 'offers wireless devices' to residents with hearing loss up to 400% of FPL (application forms dated 3-2026 and 4-2026). https://nj.gov/humanservices/ddhh/services/edp/ UNVERIFIED: measured caption word error rate vs indoor signal on Verizon at Rutgers. That is our primary data.
- **Verizon tech:** IMS media plane (MRF/SBC) ASR running on Verizon's side before the RAN downlink, optionally on Verizon 5G Edge MEC for latency. Captions are delivered as text on the VoLTE call's RTT channel (RFC 4103 with redundancy), which every RTT-capable phone renders natively. VoLTE and Wi-Fi Calling paths. Subscriber opt-in is an accessibility flag in the IMS profile. Optional TRS Fund certification as an ASR-only IP CTS provider (47 CFR 64.606), or a wholesale partnership with a certified provider.
- **Verizon money:** New revenue that is not a subsidy: ASR-only IP CTS pays $0.95 per captioned minute from a $1.56B federal fund that Verizon already contributes to (2.276% factor). Formula with labeled assumptions: Verizon hard-of-hearing users who opt in x captioned minutes per month x $0.95 x 12, less ASR compute. Example only: 100k users x 100 min x $0.95 x 12 = about $114M a year; the minutes are UNVERIFIED and must be measured. Retention: hearing loss is concentrated in older, high-tenure postpaid customers, and 1 bp of churn is about 90,000 net adds (Schulman, Jan 30 2026). Channel: state equipment programs such as NJ DDHH's now hand out wireless devices, a ready on-ramp for a 'captions built in' Verizon line. Honest caveat: the revenue depends on FCC certification and per-user registration rules.
- **Dated anchor:** FCC DA 26-646 (Jun 30 2026): 2026-27 IP CTS rates of $0.95 ASR-only and $1.45 CA-assisted, a $1.563B fund, and a 0.02276 contribution factor. FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083 (Oct 7 2026): caption inaccuracy, forwarding failures that blocked captioning, and the call for a 'one number solution'. NJ DDHH EDP moved to 'wireless devices' (forms dated Mar/Apr 2026).
- **Primary data:** (1) A campus caption-grade walk test at about 30 Rutgers spots: basements, new glass buildings, dorm rooms, lecture halls, SHI concourses. A far-end phone in good coverage plays standard sentence lists. The receiving Verizon phone's call audio is captured through a wired USB-C audio interface. Whisper WER on the received audio, our proxy for on-device captions, is compared with WER on the clean source audio, our proxy for network-side captions. RSRP, RSRQ and SINR are logged at each spot. In parallel we send an RTT/SMS text burst to measure whether text gets through where voice fails. (2) Calls into Rutgers' Teams/PSTN lines to measure how narrowband interconnect hurts caption accuracy. (3) A consented reading panel of hard-of-hearing students through ODS that rates both caption streams. No one else has a signal-vs-caption-accuracy map of a campus.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT (20 s): Split screen. Left: a student in a basement study room on a call. The voice breaks up and the phone's own live captions turn 'meet me at the Busch dining hall at six' into nonsense. Right: the same call with network captions. The sentence arrives whole as text, because it was transcribed before the radio. Overlay: our measured WER at the 10 worst campus spots, on-device vs network. Closing line: 'Your number. Your phone. Captions that survive the basement.' POSTER TABLE: a packet-loss knob (netem on the voice leg of our emulated IMS) that visitors turn up. The voice and the receiver-side captions degrade, while the RTT caption stream from the pre-radio ASR stays readable. Next to it, the campus WER-vs-RSRQ map.
- **Why unique:** LLM-default teams would build 'AR captions' (AccessED already won with that in 2024-25) or an app. The insight here is network-native: the network sees the audio before the user's radio damages it, and the RTT channel is a robust caption pipe already in every phone. Teams that do not read the 2026 TRS rate order will not know about the $0.95/min money or Verizon's contribution, and nobody has campus caption-accuracy data.
- **Biggest risk:** Regulatory path: certification as an IP CTS provider, user self-certification and registration, and the TRS confidentiality rules on running ASR over call audio. Captions arriving as RTT text from the 'other party' may confuse users unless labeled. Free on-device captions are good enough in good coverage, so the gain exists only where signal is poor, and our data must show that gap is large. Feasibility: the IMS insertion is emulated off-network. T-Mobile's long history in relay services (UNVERIFIED for 2026) could pre-empt it.

## I03: ACCESS PRONG: the accessibility check the FCC deleted on Oct 15, 2026, run by Verizon on every line it moves (lens accessibility, pillar Pillar 2: Cost & Sustainability Transformation (copper retirement with less delay and fewer truck rolls), with Pillar 1 automation of the nightly test, self-score 61)

**One-liner:** Until Oct 15, 2026, a carrier seeking automatic approval to discontinue copper voice had to show the replacement complied with the accessibility rules for telecom, voicemail and interactive menus. FCC 26-19 removed that showing, while Verizon is retiring copper in 62 NJ wire centers. ACCESS PRONG is Verizon's voluntary replacement for it. A low-cost harness tests TTY (Baudot) round trips, voice-carry-over and captioned-phone paths through Verizon's copper replacements (Business Phone Connect, One Talk, 5G/LTE Business Internet voice). It gives every migrated accessibility line a network-confirmed install, so a blind, Deaf or low-dexterity customer is never told to 'check the light' or 'dial the code and listen'.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED: (1) The removed 47 CFR 63.602(b)(2)(iii) required the replacement to comply 'with regulations governing accessibility, usability, and compatibility requirements for: (A) Telecommunications services and functionalities; (B) Voicemail and interactive menu functionalities; and (C) Advanced communications services'. FCC 26-19 removed 63.602 effective Oct 15 2026 (91 FR 62334). eCFR text is saved at /home/user/verizonsmartcampus/research/sources/ecfr_47cfr63.602_as_of_2026-10-01_removed_2026-10-15.txt and the rule at https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/10/01/2026-20192. The underlying Part 6/7/14 duties still apply, but nobody checks them at cutover. (2) The same order removed the 63.71(a)(6) notices that the replacement 'may not provide line power' and 'steps ... to ensure safe use' (founder-verified). (3) Verizon NJ notice 2026-03-A-NJ (Sept 8 2026) retires copper in 62 wire centers including Piscataway, 'on or after December 10, 2026'. https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/verizon-new-jersey-copper-retirement-notice-2026-0908.pdf The FCC pulled the NJ-inclusive voice discontinuance (WC 26-227) from auto-grant on Sept 25 2026 (DA 26-1038). https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1038A1.pdf (4) Verizon's BPC100 support page says TTY is set by lifting the handset and dialing a code, with modes '0 (TTY Off), 1 (TTY Full), 2 (VCO), and 3 (HCO)'. It says nothing on 911, RTT or which TTYs work. https://www.verizon.com/support/knowledge-base-229252 (5) The BPC100 runs on 3 AA batteries (23 h standby, 3 h talk). https://www.verizon.com/business/shop/products/devices/connected-devices/inseego-business-phone-connect/ (6) FCC 26-56 (effective Oct 13 2026) dropped the ASCII requirement for TTY-based TRS. https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/09/10/2026-18478/telecommunications-relay-service-ascii-format-requirement UNVERIFIED: whether Baudot survives Verizon's VoLTE replacements end to end, and whether the BPC100's TTY-mode confirmation is audible-only.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon Business Phone Connect (Inseego BPC100: VoLTE, TTY Full/VCO/HCO modes, Verizon MDM), One Talk, LTE/5G Business Internet voice, ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M line ($1.50-$6/mo) for a nightly TTY-test supervisor, ThingSpace Connectivity Management/Device Diagnostics callbacks for the 'install confirmed' event, and the copper retirement program (2026-03-A-NJ). The network-confirmed install: when the gateway registers, Verizon places an automated confirmation to the customer in the customer's own mode (voice, TTY or RTT text to a mobile) stating the line works and the 911 address on file.
- **Verizon money:** (a) Approval speed. 26-227 is paused for review after objections about vulnerable and emergency users. A documented accessibility-verified migration is the evidence that answers them, and each month a wire center stays on copper keeps its O&M. Our research record gives about $180M/yr saved from 36 COs (Verizon, 2022), roughly $5M per CO-year. The ratio is our arithmetic, applied as a labeled assumption to the 62 NJ wire centers. (b) Truck rolls. AICC reports up to 3-hour truck rolls with about 25% no-access on alarm migrations (https://tma.us/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AICC-Petition-for-Emergency-Relief-5-10-21-FINAL.pdf). Network-confirmed self-install for customers who cannot read LEDs avoids a share of those visits; Verizon's per-roll cost is UNVERIFIED. (c) Retention of older landline-replacement customers with disabilities, a low-churn base, at a forced-migration moment. No copper savings are credited to us beyond the delay avoided.
- **Dated anchor:** FCC 26-19 amendments effective Oct 15 2026: 63.602, with its accessibility prong, removed (91 FR 62334, Oct 1 2026). Verizon NJ copper notice 2026-03-A-NJ (Sept 8 2026; on or after Dec 10 2026). DA 26-1038 (Sept 25 2026) paused the NJ voice discontinuance. FCC 26-56 (TTY ASCII) effective Oct 13 2026. NJ DDHH EDP forms dated 3-2026 and 4-2026.
- **Primary data:** (1) A bench lab, the first independent one: Baudot 45.45-baud TTY round trips using the open-source minimodem TDD mode through a phone-line audio interface, our own TTY-to-TTY lines only, never 911 or relay. We measure character error rate across a Rutgers OIT analog line, a Verizon BPC100 on VoLTE, and One Talk, at three RSRQ levels found in Rutgers basements and machine rooms. Voice-carry-over and hearing-carry-over timing too. (2) Usability: can a Deaf user confirm the BPC100 TTY mode without hearing a tone; can a blind or low-dexterity user swap its AA batteries and confirm service. Consented sessions through ODS and Rutgers' Osher Lifelong Learning Institute (older adults who still keep landlines). (3) An OPRA request to NJ DHS DDHH for equipment-distribution counts by municipality, overlaid on the 62 retiring NJ wire centers. A full map is a Feb 3 poster item if the response is slow. (4) Rutgers' own exposure: ODS's published fax 732.445.3388 sits on a Verizon Piscataway 5ESS NXX. We ask OIT for the remaining TTY, fax and analog accessibility lines.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT (20 s): A TTY prints 'HELLO THIS IS A TEST GA' cleanly over copper. Flip the path switch to the copper replacement and the measured result appears, either 'CER 0.4%' or 'CER 31%: unusable'. Caption: 'Oct 15, 2026: the FCC stopped asking whether replacements are accessible.' Then a blind tester plugs in the replacement gateway and her phone announces: 'Verizon: your new line works; 911 address 12 Hoes Lane.' No light to read. POSTER TABLE: the live harness with a three-way path toggle and live CER bars, plus the NJ map of retiring wire centers.
- **Why unique:** Nobody, LLM-default teams included, knows the accessibility prong existed, let alone that it vanished on Oct 15, 2026, six days before we started. The bench data is new and cheap. It is the accessibility counterpart to our Last Copper champion (homes and offices, not elevators). It differs from the explored HELP Line, which made elevator HELP buttons Deaf-accessible.
- **Biggest risk:** Overlaps the customer-experience lens's 'Still Works?' device check, which also lists TTY. Ours is narrower: disability devices and disabled installers, anchored on the deleted accessibility prong. TTY use is small and shrinking (FCC 26-56 says so), which weakens Ubiquity. Verizon's BPC100 already claims TTY modes, so the measured answer may be 'it works', which turns the pitch into verification rather than rescue. Testing captioned phones legitimately requires an eligible user. The OPRA reply may miss Nov 8.

## I04: ONE NUMBER: a Deaf customer's relay calls and texts live on one Verizon number, and Verizon care never refuses a relay call again (lens accessibility, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services to Enhance Customer Experience, self-score 53)

**One-liner:** Signing Deaf people typically carry two phone numbers: a relay number from their video relay provider and a mobile number for texts. Hearing callers dial the wrong one, texts go to the relay number and vanish, and carrier care lines refuse VRS calls because they cannot verify a third party. The FCC recorded all three on Oct 7, 2026. ONE NUMBER lets Verizon's network route voice calls to the customer's chosen relay provider while texts and RTT stay on the phone. The network attests the relay call as coming from the account holder, so Verizon care can take it like any other call.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED from FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083 (Oct 7 2026), https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1083A1.pdf: (1) 'Individuals who are deaf, deafblind, hard of hearing, or have speech disabilities, must typically utilize at least two different telephone numbers ... Having two numbers ... risks texts being sent to a relay service and calls being placed to a number only used for text services.' Consumer groups ask for a 'one number solution'. (2) RDAs 'addressed recurrent problems where carriers refused to accept Video Relay Service (VRS) relay calls from deaf users ... alongside call forwarding failures that blocked access to captioning or accessible communication.' (3) 47 CFR 64.611(a)(1) creates the split: on registration a VRS provider must either port the user's number or 'assign that user a geographically appropriate ... telephone number', and must 'route and deliver all of that user's inbound and outbound calls'. https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-47/chapter-I/subchapter-B/part-64/subpart-F/section-64.611 (4) VRS pays $6.96/min (Tier I) in 2026-27, so relay minutes are valuable, metered traffic (DA 26-646). https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-646A1.pdf
- **Verizon tech:** IMS subscriber profile (HSS) with an accessibility routing flag. A telephony application server forwards inbound PSTN voice to the customer's registered VRS or IP CTS provider by SIP URI, looked up from the iTRS Numbering Directory, while SMS, RCS and RTT terminate on the handset. STIR/SHAKEN full attestation and a network identity token are passed on the relay leg so care, banks or Rutgers offices can trust that the relay call is from the account holder. Verizon care integration accepts network-attested relay calls without third-party refusal.
- **Verizon money:** Deaf and hard-of-hearing customer acquisition and retention, a segment where carrier choice turns on accessibility features. Formula with labeled assumptions: addressable signing Deaf mobile users (UNVERIFIED; on the order of hundreds of thousands) x share Verizon wins x postpaid ARPA. Care cost: fewer failed and repeated relay contacts. Regulatory: fewer accessibility RDAs. Every 1 bp of churn is about 90,000 net adds (Schulman, Jan 30 2026). Honest size: small. This is brand and segment leadership, not a large P&L line.
- **Dated anchor:** FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083, released Oct 7 2026: the 'one number solution' request and RDAs over carriers refusing VRS calls and forwarding failures blocking captioning. FCC DA 26-646 (Jun 30 2026) VRS and IP CTS rates.
- **Primary data:** (1) A consented survey and interviews with Deaf, hard-of-hearing and DeafBlind students and staff (through ODS, Rutgers ASL courses and clubs, NJ DDHH) on how many numbers they keep, which they give out, and how often texts or calls are lost. (2) With one Deaf collaborator making genuine calls about her own accounts (hearing team members never use relay): placing VRS calls to Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile care, and to five Rutgers offices, recording accepted, refused or 'need to verify' outcomes. (3) A test of Verizon conditional call forwarding to captioning and relay numbers, run with an eligible volunteer, to reproduce or rule out the FCC's 'forwarding failures'.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT (15 s): A phone shows two contact cards for one Deaf student, 'Maya (text)' and 'Maya (VRS)'. Her professor calls the wrong one, and her friend's text goes to the relay number and disappears. Then ONE NUMBER: one card. The professor's call rings Maya's relay app with an interpreter, the text lands in Messages, and a Verizon care agent on a relay call sees 'network-verified account holder'. POSTER TABLE: a routing emulator (open-source IMS on a laptop) with a visitor-operated switch, plus survey charts and the three-carrier relay-acceptance table.
- **Why unique:** Only someone who read the FCC's Oct 7, 2026 report knows the 'one number' demand and the carrier-refusal RDAs. It is a pure network routing and identity feature, not an app. It is distinct from our explored Lifeline Video (QoS for sign-language video).
- **Biggest risk:** It needs changes to iTRS numbering and directory rules and buy-in from relay providers. It cannot be built on Verizon's real network by students, so the open-source IMS emulator invites the same 'not Verizon's network' critique that sank HangUp. The primary data depends on recruiting Deaf collaborators in 18 days. The Verizon money is small, and T-Mobile, with its relay heritage, may move first.

## I05: NO TRAY: a blind or low-dexterity customer activates a Verizon line without a SIM pin, a QR code, or reading a code aloud (lens accessibility, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services to Enhance Customer Experience (with a Pillar 2 effect on assisted-activation cost), self-score 55)

**One-liner:** The FCC's Oct 7, 2026 report records disabled consumers who could not install a physical SIM and were locked out by inaccessible verification steps. The same month, the eSIM-only foldable iPhone shipped (Oct 23) and Verizon finished moving 274 stores to franchisees. NO TRAY makes Verizon's activation sight-free and pin-free from the network side. The eSIM is pushed by carrier activation instead of a QR code to aim a camera at. The network confirms success by calling or texting the customer in their preferred mode. Each step is measured with blind, low-vision and limited-dexterity users.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED: (1) FCC DA 26-1083 (Oct 7 2026), RDAs: barriers included 'phones requiring a physical SIM card that the user was not able to successfully install on the basis of their disability', and 'many involved inaccessible multi-factor authentication (MFA) processes that prevented consumers who are blind, low-vision, aging, people with cognitive issues, or people with limited manual dexterity from accessing their accounts'. Commenters cite 'managing time-limited codes before they expire'. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1083A1.pdf (2) Verizon: the eSIM-only foldable iPhone (iPhone Duo) opened preorders Oct 16 2026, in stores Oct 23. https://www.verizon.com/about/news/es/verizon-ofertas-preventa-iphone-18-pro (3) Schulman (Jan 30 2026) names friction in onboarding, billing and customer service calls as a churn cause; 1 bp of churn is about 90,000 net adds. https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-02/vz_4q25_transcript_013026r.pdf (4) Our research record: 274 Verizon stores moved to franchisees on Aug 16 2026 (URL not re-fetched this pass).
- **Verizon tech:** eSIM remote provisioning (GSMA SGP.22 SM-DP+): carrier-push activation and eSIM transfer instead of QR scanning. A network registration event triggers an automated confirmation call or text in the user's chosen mode (voice, RTT, large-text SMS) stating the line, the number and the 911 address. Number Verification (CAMARA via Aduna) is optional for the identity step. We keep that light because our explored 'No Code' concept covered OTP replacement.
- **Verizon money:** (a) Churn: onboarding friction is a named churn cause, and 1 bp is about 90,000 net adds. Customers with vision or dexterity disabilities who fail activation return devices or churn early. (b) Cost to serve: fewer store-assisted and care-assisted activations at a time of fewer corporate stores. Formula with labeled assumptions: activations by customers needing assistance x assisted-activation cost avoided (UNVERIFIED) + early-life churn avoided x lifetime margin. (c) Regulatory: fewer accessibility RDAs on activation and MFA. Honest size: small to moderate; the value is mainly retention.
- **Dated anchor:** FCC CVAA Biennial Report DA 26-1083 (Oct 7 2026), RDAs on physical SIM installation and MFA. Verizon eSIM-only iPhone Duo (preorders Oct 16, stores Oct 23 2026). 274 stores franchised Aug 16 2026.
- **Primary data:** A consented usability study with 6-10 blind, low-vision and limited-dexterity participants, recruited through Rutgers ODS (which already runs Aira for blind students, https://ods.rutgers.edu/aira), NJ CBVI and Rutgers OLLI older adults. Each participant activates inexpensive Verizon prepaid lines by three paths: physical SIM, QR-code eSIM, and carrier-push/transfer eSIM. We record completion rate, minutes, number of assists, and each blocking step (screen-reader labels, camera aiming, time-limited codes). We also audit the activation flow with VoiceOver and TalkBack. No dataset like this exists for any US carrier.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT (15 s): Close-up of a SIM tray pin in a participant's hand, with a timer at 6:40 and counting. Cut to the same participant on the pushed-eSIM path: her phone says 'Verizon: your line is active, number ending 2219', and the timer stops at 1:50. Caption: 'FCC, Oct 7 2026: consumers locked out by physical SIMs and inaccessible verification.' Our completion-rate bars by path. POSTER TABLE: recorded sessions with consent, the step-by-step barrier map, and a live network-confirmation demo on a ThingSpace-connected device.
- **Why unique:** LLM-default teams treat eSIM as a convenience. The disability evidence comes from a report released Oct 7, 2026 and from our own usability data. It targets Verizon's own onboarding in the month Verizon's first eSIM-only foldable ships.
- **Biggest risk:** Judges may see it as CX/UX work rather than network technology, which weakens Content. Recruiting enough participants and getting consent and IRB clearance in 18 days is hard. Verizon may already offer carrier-push eSIM broadly, which would shrink the gap to measurement. Activation flows are partly Apple and Google UI that Verizon does not control. It sits near our explored 'No Code', so OTP replacement must stay out of scope.

## I06: Leaf-Off: Verizon qualifies the home in November, and the trees take the signal back in May (lens contrarian, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services / Customer Experience (fewer FWA and copper-replacement disappointments), with Pillar 1 (an AI-learned seasonal correction applied automatically inside qualification), self-score 60)

**One-liner:** Verizon decides whether a home can live on wireless (5G Home, LTE Home, or the wireless home phone that replaces copper) from a signal reading taken on the day of qualification or self-setup. In New Jersey's leaf-off months that reading goes through bare branches. When the canopy comes back, C-band loses a few dB (NTIA measured 1.8 to 2.9 dB at 3.475 GHz in a suburb) and mmWave loses much more, and by then the return window has closed. Leaf fall is happening now, inside our Round-1 window. Rutgers students measure the same Verizon links from fixed tripods under campus tree cover before, during and after leaf fall, fit dB lost against canopy fraction for each band, and give Verizon two things: a seasonal margin for address qualification, and a self-setup warning ('order the outdoor receiver now'). Indoor-coverage proofs ask where the signal fails. Leaf-Off asks when.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) Verizon's Q2 2026 8-K (Jul 24, 2026): FWA broadband connections reached 6,208K (+21.4% YoY), but FWA net adds fell to 193K from 278K (-30.6%), and H1 2026 to 407K from 586K (-30.5%). https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/732712/000073271226000040/a2026q2exhibit99.htm (2) Customers already tell the FCC that trees break the wireless replacement. In our ECFS pull of WC 26-227 (Oct 21, 2026; 615 filings, 427 with text), 40 written comments mention trees, foliage or leaves. Culpeper County, VA (Sept 8, 2026, ECFS 26110075707): 'Mobile wireless alternatives will not work reliably in our area of Culpeper County with weak or no cell signal, especially during full foliage seasons or storm weather.' Independence Township, Warren County, NJ (Sept 3, 2026, ECFS 26110075397): 'There is no cell service in my area ... (we have a lot of trees)'. Another filer (Sept 18, 2026): 'I am surrounded by trees. I have no way to call 911 other than my landline.' A form letter filed 12 times cites 'heavy tree canopy' and says 'Outdoor paper coverage maps do not reflect my indoor reality.' The commenters also say Verizon came to test signal at their homes. https://www.fcc.gov/ecfs/search/docket-detail/26-227 (API: https://publicapi.fcc.gov/ecfs/filings?proceedings.name=26-227) (3) The federal test has no season. FCC 26-19, effective Oct 15, 2026, new 47 CFR 63.71(f)(2)(ii) auto-grants where mobile wireless at 5/1 Mbps is available 'consistent with the coverage parameters set forth in § 1.7004(c)(3)', which are modelled map parameters. https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf (4) The physics is real but under-modelled. In NTIA/ITS (Kozma, Hicks and Lain, USNC-URSI NRSM, Jan 2024), suburban drive tests on Dec 16, 2022 and Jun 21, 2023 found summer median clutter loss higher by 2.86 dB and 1.76 dB at 3475 MHz, and by 3.71 dB and 2.83 dB at 3175 MHz, for the two transmitters. The authors write that 'an overlooked component of clutter model development is time variability' and that the effect 'disproportionately affects higher elevation angle paths'. https://its.ntia.gov/publications/download/Kozma_Clutter_URSI2024.pdf (5) At mmWave the seasonal swing is far larger. A NASA tree-fade study at 20 GHz reported a median fade of about 23 dB with leaves versus about 7 dB without (from a search summary, not re-read): https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19940010199/downloads/19940010199.pdf. Vegetation model: ITU-R P.833, https://www.itu.int/rec/R-REC-P.833 UNVERIFIED: whether Verizon's qualification engine or gateway signal meter applies a leaf-on margin; FWA churn by install month; FWA-specific return-window terms.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon 5G Home and 5G Business Internet on C-band (n77) and mmWave (n261), LTE Home Internet, Verizon's wireless home-phone copper replacement, the gateway's self-setup signal guidance, mmWave and C-band outdoor receivers, and the address-qualification engine (predicted signal plus sector capacity). Student kit: one Verizon FWA gateway plus two 5G phones logging RSRP, RSRQ, SINR, the serving PCI and band, and throughput, on surveyed tripod marks (about 10) whose path to an identified Verizon site runs through deciduous canopy, with an open-sky control mark for each site. Canopy fraction comes from daily fisheye photos (the ecology 'gap fraction' method). Output: a per-band seasonal margin model (dB per unit of canopy, by elevation angle), packaged as one extra input to qualification and as a rule for the self-setup app ('this address loses X dB in leaf-on months: ship the outdoor unit').
- **Verizon money:** This moves Consumer and Business fixed-wireless revenue (6.2M FWA connections), broadband churn, and the mobility churn of converged households (Schulman, Jan 30, 2026: bundled customers churn about 40% less; each basis point of churn is about 90,000 net adds; https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-02/vz_4q25_transcript_013026r.pdf). Formula with labeled assumptions: avoided churn revenue per year = G x f x p x c x ARPU x 12. ASSUMED: G = 1.0M FWA gross adds/yr; f = 40% installed November to April; p = 10% of those inside the measured leaf-on margin; c = 25% of marginal homes churn early; ARPU = $50. That is about 10,000 homes and about $6M/yr retained, before counting returned gateways, care calls, repeat dispatches and the mobility lines that leave with a broadband churner. Second line, copper exit: a copper customer moved to wireless in winter whose service fails in June becomes a repeat dispatch, a complaint of exactly the kind that got WC 26-227 pulled from auto-grant (DA 26-1038, Sept 25, 2026), or a reversion to copper. Honest view: every input except the 6.2M base is an assumption, and the size of the C-band effect at Rutgers is the open question. The cheapest win may simply be shipping the outdoor receiver up front to the addresses the model flags.
- **Dated anchor:** Jul 24, 2026: Verizon's own 8-K shows FWA net adds down 30.6% YoY. Aug 28 to Oct 19, 2026: WC 26-227 comments citing 'full foliage seasons' and 'heavy tree canopy'. Sept 25, 2026: DA 26-1038 pulls the application from auto-grant. Oct 15, 2026: FCC 26-19's seasonless 5/1 Mbps mobile-wireless test takes effect. Late Oct to mid Nov 2026: New Jersey leaf fall, a natural experiment that exists only during the Round-1 window.
- **Primary data:** (1) Daily fixed-point C-band and mmWave measurements at about 10 tripod marks (Busch, the Livingston Ecological Preserve edge, Cook/Douglass, College Ave) from Oct 22, 2026 through full leaf-off, then monthly through the Feb 3 poster (bare and snow conditions) and again in May 2027 at leaf-on. Same marks, same tower, same antenna height give a full annual curve. (2) Canopy fraction per mark from daily fisheye photos, validated with a Rutgers ecology lab. We will ask for access to Rutgers' Hutcheson Memorial Forest, an old-growth deciduous stand near Busch, as a high-canopy control site (access UNVERIFIED). (3) An anonymous census of off-campus student houses on Verizon FWA: install month, seasonal slowdowns, complaints, and whether they bought an outdoor unit. No personal data, and no subscriber data from Verizon.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): 0:00 the Culpeper quote on screen: 'especially during full foliage seasons'. 0:12 a time-lapse of a Busch oak dropping its leaves beside a live SINR trace climbing. 0:30 'Same tripod, same Verizon site: Oct 22 versus Nov 7, +X dB' (measured), next to NTIA's 1.8-2.9 dB. 0:50 flip forward: 'What a November customer gets in May' (model, labeled as a forecast). 1:10 the qualification mock-up flags an address 'leaf-on risk: ship the outdoor receiver' and the self-setup screen warns the user. 1:30 Verizon's FWA net-adds chart from its 8-K and the churn formula. 1:50 close: 'Qualify for June, not for November.' POSTER TABLE (Feb 3): a live gateway on a tripod reading the Verizon site behind the building, the measured dB-versus-canopy curve, and the spring forecast that we promise to check in May.
- **Why unique:** No one pitches trees to a telecom company, and it sounds trivial. But it is defensible: NTIA calls time variability an overlooked part of clutter modelling, the FCC's new replacement test has no season, and Verizon's own customers are telling the FCC about 'full foliage seasons'. The effect can be measured only by people who are on site every day during leaf fall, and that window is the next three weeks. It is not an app, a twin or energy, and network planners will recognize the RF immediately.
- **Biggest risk:** Verizon's planning tools may already assume leaf-on clutter, and a judge may say 'we design for summer'. The C-band difference may be small (1-3 dB), leaving mmWave, a minority of FWA, as the strong story. The link from season to churn is inferred, because no Verizon churn-by-month data is public. The leaf-on return is observable only in May, after the finals. Tripod placement needs Facilities permission. It sits next to other teams' indoor-coverage proof ideas, so it must stay about time, not place.

## I07: Every Objection Is a Trouble Ticket: the letters Verizon's copper exit is stuck on, turned into a network-planning worklist (lens contrarian, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (an objection-to-ticket agent), serving Pillar 2 (the copper/TDM exit is a named proof point of Verizon's cost program), self-score 55)

**One-liner:** On Sept 25, 2026 the FCC pulled Verizon's 9-state legacy-voice exit, New Jersey included, out of automatic grant. The docket now holds 615 filings. We read all of them with code. Of the 427 with written text, 80% mention cell coverage or power: that is a network-planning problem, not a policy argument. An AI pipeline clusters form letters, classifies each objection, ties it to a town and wire center, and proposes a fix: outdoor FWA receiver, Network Extender, battery kit, small-cell ticket, or 'keep the copper here'. Students verify New Jersey cases in the field from public places. Verizon gets an evidence pack per objection class, and its planners get a geocoded worklist for free.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) DA 26-890 (Aug 26, 2026), WC 26-227: Verizon New Jersey Inc. and affiliates applied to discontinue residential and business legacy TDM voice in portions of AZ, DE, NJ, NM, OH, RI, SC, UT and VA, on or after Oct 23, 2026. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-890A1.pdf (2) DA 26-1038 (Sept 25, 2026): the application 'will not be automatically granted. The Commission requires additional time to complete its review'. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1038A1.pdf (3) Our ECFS API pull (Oct 21, 2026) found 615 filings dated Aug 10 to Oct 19, 2026, 427 of them with comment text and 410 distinct openings (the largest form letter has 12 copies). First-pass keyword counts, to be hand-validated by two coders: 300 (70%) mention cell service, signal or coverage; 192 (45%) power outages or batteries; 341 (80%) one or both; 276 (65%) 911 or emergencies; 105 (25%) age; 77 (18%) medical devices; 31 (7%) alarms; 158 (37%) rural, wooded or hilly terrain; 40 (9%) trees or foliage. https://www.fcc.gov/ecfs/search/docket-detail/26-227 (API: https://publicapi.fcc.gov/ecfs/filings?proceedings.name=26-227) (4) Institutional filers include the NJ Board of Public Utilities' Office of Cable TV & Telecommunications, the Borough of Franklin Lakes and the Township of Hanover (all Sept 10), Shamong Township, NJ (Sept 8: 'a rural community that lacks the broadband infrastructure'), the Virginia SCC, the WV PSC, about 15 Virginia counties and the Virginia Association of Counties, Rep. Suhas Subramanyam (Sept 3) and the Association of BellTel Retirees (Sept 23). Verizon filed reply comments on Sept 24 and an ex parte notice on Oct 9, and Rappahannock County filed an ex parte on Oct 16 (ECFS metadata; the PDFs were not read because ECFS returns 403 to our fetcher). (5) New Jersey residents wrote from Middletown (Sept 9: 'cell phone service is abysmal') and Independence Township, Warren County (Sept 3: 'There is no cell service in my area'). One filer says Verizon 'tested it here and found the signal is not strong enough for a non-copper line phone' (Sept 8). Another says the notice 'did not include the FCC docket number' (Sept 18). So Verizon already measures house by house; what is missing is aggregation and a fix. (6) FCC 26-19, effective Oct 15, 2026, new 47 CFR 63.71(f)(2)(ii): auto-grant where every location has mobile wireless at 5/1 Mbps 'consistent with the coverage parameters set forth in § 1.7004(c)(3)', which are modelled, not measured indoors. https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf
- **Verizon tech:** The replacement paths Verizon itself relies on for the 63.71(f)(2) showing: Verizon LTE/5G voice (VoLTE) and Verizon's wireless home-phone replacement, LTE/5G Home Internet (FWA) with an outdoor receiver where indoor signal fails, the LTE Network Extender, Fios Digital Voice where fiber passes, and small cells where demand clusters. The student field kit is Verizon phones logging RSRP, RSRQ and SINR, VoLTE call setup and completion to a team number, and the 5/1 Mbps test, indoors and outdoors at public sites. The pipeline is an LLM classifier with form-letter clustering and a geocoder, run over public ECFS data with no CPNI. Each output ticket is in network-planning format: place, symptom, measured KPI, recommended fix and cost class.
- **Verizon money:** This moves cost of services (copper and TDM O&M, inside FY2025 cost of services of $27.8B) and the timing of the opex program in which Skiadas named copper decommissioning, recycling and monetization (Q1'26 call, Apr 27, 2026: https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-06/vz_1q26_transcript_042726rm.pdf). Benchmark: the 2022 program saved about $180M/yr from 36 COs, or about $5M per CO-year (derived; https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2022-03/Investor-Day-2022-Transcript.pdf). Formula with labeled assumptions: value of a shorter pause = S x W x m/12. ASSUMED: S = $1M per wire center-year (20% of the benchmark); W = 62 NJ wire centers (the Sept 8 notice; how far 26-227 overlaps them is UNVERIFIED); m = 2 months saved. That gives about $10M; across the 297 legacy-Verizon wire centers in the Sept 2026 notices it is about $50M. A second line: objectors are paying Verizon voice customers (one writes 'I pay $100 a month for my landline'), and moving them to Verizon wireless, FWA or Fios keeps them instead of losing them to cable. Honest view: Verizon Regulatory already reads and answers this docket (Sept 24, Oct 9). Our increment is speed, one consistent taxonomy, geocoding, field evidence, and turning complaints into planning tickets, including an explicit 'keep copper here' carve-out list where the objector is right.
- **Dated anchor:** Aug 26, 2026 (DA 26-890); Sept 8-10, 2026 (NJ BPU, NJ township and Virginia county comments); Sept 25, 2026 (DA 26-1038 pulls auto-grant); Oct 9, 2026 (Verizon ex parte); Oct 15, 2026 (FCC 26-19 takes effect with the 5/1 Mbps test); Oct 16-19, 2026 (newest filings); data pulled Oct 21, 2026. An LLM-default team cannot know any of these.
- **Primary data:** (1) A hand-validated, double-coded taxonomy of all filings in 26-227 (Cohen's kappa reported), refreshed weekly until the Feb 3 poster, plus the new master objection docket WC 26-214. (2) Field measurements at public places only (libraries, municipal buildings, post-office lots) in the NJ townships the commenters name (Middletown, Independence, Mansfield, Franklin Lakes, Hanover, Shamong) and in wire centers from the Sept 8 NJ notice within 90 minutes of campus (Hackettstown, Phillipsburg, Browntown, Neshanic). Each point gets indoor and outdoor 5/1 Mbps, SINR and 10 VoLTE test calls to a team number. We never dial 911 and never contact commenters. (3) A bench test of how long Verizon's replacement devices stay up on battery against vendor claims, which answers the 45% of comments about power.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): 0:00 a wall of 615 letters. 0:15 the agent sorts them live: form letters collapse, then piles form for 'signal' 70%, 'power' 45%, '911' 65%. 0:35 one pin in Warren County, NJ, with the commenter's words on screen. 0:50 two students in a township library lot with three Verizon phones: SINR, a VoLTE call, 5/1 pass or fail, indoors versus outdoors. 1:20 the objection becomes a ticket in a mock Verizon planning queue: 'outdoor FWA receiver + battery kit' or 'keep copper: carve-out'. 1:45 close: 'Every objection is a free trouble ticket.' POSTER TABLE: a dashboard refreshed from the ECFS API, the NJ field map, the battery bench rig, and one resolution pack per objection class.
- **Why unique:** It sounds wrong, because students are helping a carrier answer complaints. It is defensible because 80% of the complaints are about coverage or power, which a network can fix, and the rest go on an honest carve-out list. The data and the pause are post-cutoff and live. It is not a lifeline-device product or an app, and it speaks the language of Verizon's GN&T Transformation Office (copper-exit timing).
- **Biggest risk:** Optics: it could look like helping Verizon override frightened elderly rural customers, so it must be framed as 'fix it or keep the copper'. Most objectors are in Virginia (104 texts mention Virginia versus 7 New Jersey), so NJ field work covers only a slice. Keyword counts are noisy until hand-coded. Which NJ areas 26-227 covers is UNVERIFIED. The dollar value rests on assumptions, and Verizon Regulatory may already do this internally. It also sits in the copper-exit space where most LLM-assisted teams, and several of our own lenses, have converged.

## I08: Never Attached: the network knows a phone was trafficked weeks before the first missed payment (lens contrarian, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (an anomaly model on registration and order events that acts by itself), with Pillar 2 (bad-debt and fraud cost), self-score 50)

**One-liner:** Verizon told the FCC it lost about 784,703 devices to fraud in 2023, costing 'hundreds of millions of dollars'. A trafficked phone behaves differently on the network from the first day. After store activation it never registers again with its own SIM, or that SIM turns up in a different handset, while the same account keeps ordering phones. Never Attached turns that first-week network silence into an automatic hold on the account's next device order and a step-up identity check, weeks before the first bill is due. Rutgers students supply the part Verizon cannot see: how fraud rings recruit students to open lines for cash, measured by an anonymous census, a catalog of recruitment posts, and a weekly count of new locked phones on local resale listings.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) FCC DA 26-43 (Jan 12, 2026), waiving Verizon's 60-day unlocking rule: 'Verizon claims that this has culminated in it losing an estimated 784,703 devices to fraud across both prepaid and postpaid offerings in 2023, costing it hundreds of millions of dollars.' The order describes 'criminal actors using stolen credentials to pass a credit check through identity theft or synthetic fraud, then default on the payments and resell the handset on the black market', a resale premium 'of up to $300 or more', and more than 46 robberies of DC-area Verizon stores in 12 months. https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-43A1.pdf (2) Verizon FY2025 10-K (Feb 17, 2026): credit-loss provisions of $1,622M on device receivables plus $492M on service ($2,114M). Customers with under 210 days of tenure caused $884M of the $1,356M device write-offs (about 65%) while holding about 18% of receivables. A 0.25% change in the loss rate moves bad-debt expense by $167M. https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/732712/000073271226000007/vz-20251231.htm (3) Verizon Q2 2026 10-Q (Jul 31, 2026): H1 2026 credit-loss provision of $653M (device) plus $251M (service). https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/732712/000073271226000046/vz-20260630.htm (4) Students are a known recruitment pool. UTSA police (Oct 2017) warned of people posing as Apple employees offering a paid 'survey' that 'is actually a credit application to open numerous phone lines', leaving victims 'stuck with the phone lines and related charges': https://www.ksat.com/consumer/2017/10/04/utsa-police-warn-people-about-scam-targeting-students/. TCU (2017): a non-student offered cash to students who bought iPhones on credit: https://www.tcu360.com/2017/03/iphone-scammer-on-campus/. UK Action Fraud describes fraudsters 'offering students cash incentives to sign up for personal mobile phone contracts': https://www.actionfraud.police.uk/news/police-arrest-fraudster-over-complex-mobile-phone-scam-targeting-students UNVERIFIED: any 2026 US report of student recruitment for phone lines (none found in one search); what Verizon's fraud systems already do with registration data; Verizon's 2025-2026 device-fraud count.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon's core network registration records (MME/AMF attach and registration events) and the IMEI each handset reports at registration, checked by the equipment identity register (EIR / 5G-EIR) and the GSMA device blocklist. These are joined with order and fulfilment events from Verizon's device-financing systems. For the student prototype on real Verizon lines: ThingSpace self-serve lines ($1.50-$6/mo, verified) and the ThingSpace Connectivity Management API's connection history and device identifiers (ICCID/IMEI pairing; whether a self-serve account exposes IMEI changes is UNVERIFIED, and the fallback is a device-side log). Where available, the CAMARA SIM Swap and Device Swap network APIs (Verizon availability through Aduna UNVERIFIED). Legal basis: 47 USC 222(d)(2) allows a carrier to use its own network data to protect itself from fraud. Students never see CPNI; the model is trained and scored on synthetic event streams with fraud patterns taken from DA 26-43.
- **Verizon money:** This moves bad-debt expense (FY2025 provision $2.11B; device write-offs $1,356M, $884M of them from new customers) and the fraud losses Verizon puts at 'hundreds of millions' a year, plus store-robbery and logistics costs. Formula with labeled assumptions: avoided loss per year = D x s x v. ASSUMED: D = 784,703 fraud devices/yr (Verizon's 2023 figure); s = 10% prevented, mostly the second through fifth devices on multi-line fraud accounts that are held once the first device goes silent; v = $600 net loss per device. That is about $47M/yr, or $24M to $94M for s = 5-20%. Equivalently, a 0.03 point drop in the device loss rate is worth about $20M (from Verizon's own $167M per 0.25%). Honest view: Verizon's fraud team very likely already uses some activation signals, so the real value is the increment over their baseline, which only Verizon can measure. False positives (gift phones, phones bought for relatives abroad, phones kept in a drawer) must be handled with a soft hold and a quick ID check, never a cancellation.
- **Dated anchor:** Jul 31, 2026: Verizon's Q2 10-Q shows $904M of credit-loss provisions in H1 2026. Jan 12, 2026: DA 26-43 puts Verizon's 784,703-device fraud figure on the FCC record and replaces the 60-day lock with unlock-when-paid. The Rutgers census and resale counts are collected in Oct-Nov 2026 and do not exist anywhere else.
- **Primary data:** (1) An anonymous Rutgers student census (IRB review requested): have you been offered money to open a phone line, buy a phone on a payment plan, or 'take a survey' that asked for your SSN; did you accept; who was left with the debt. (2) A catalog of public recruitment posts aimed at New Jersey students (screenshots with all personal details removed). (3) A weekly count of new, sealed, carrier-locked phones on public resale listings within 25 miles of New Brunswick, with asking price against retail: the local resale premium. (4) A bench test on our own Verizon lines and two handsets: how fast a SIM moved between devices becomes visible in what Verizon's APIs expose. No customer data, and we never contact sellers.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): 0:00 the FCC quote '784,703 devices' on screen. 0:15 a redacted recruitment post: 'Get $300. We pay your bill.' 0:30 two timelines side by side: a real customer's phone registers every day; a trafficked phone goes silent after activation, and its SIM appears in a $30 flip phone on day 1. 0:50 the model flags the account at hour 72; its next three device orders are held automatically and an ID check is sent. 1:10 the bad-debt formula from Verizon's own 10-K numbers. 1:30 census result: 'X% of Rutgers students we asked have been offered cash to open a phone line.' 1:50 close: 'The network knows first. Let it tell finance.' POSTER TABLE: two handsets and one Verizon SIM. A visitor swaps the SIM and watches the dashboard flag the broken pairing.
- **Why unique:** Fraud operations sound boring and off-brief for a smart-campus contest, and no LLM-default team will pick them. But it uses Verizon's own number from an FCC order, treats the network as the sensor, and puts the campus where the mules are recruited. It also protects students, who are left with the debt.
- **Biggest risk:** Verizon may already do most of this, and the judges are network leaders, not fraud or finance. After the $46.9M location fine, any use of network data needs careful framing (fraud prevention under 222(d)(2), no location, no students touching CPNI). The demo runs on synthetic data plus a small real-line bench test, which weakens Content. The census may find little recruitment at Rutgers, and the recruitment evidence we have is from 2017 and the UK. False positives would hurt real customers if the hold were too blunt.

## I09: Lost Time: Verizon's C-band runs on GPS time, and 2026 showed how easily GPS goes away (lens contrarian, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (anomaly detection on interference logs and a timing-risk score per site), with Pillar 2 (targeted sync capex instead of a blanket upgrade), self-score 49)

**One-liner:** Every TDD cell must hold its frame timing within microseconds of its neighbours, and many sites take that time from GPS. In 2026 GPS loss became a US story: in May an air-ambulance crew lost GPS during a military jamming exercise in New Mexico before a fatal crash, and on Sept 25 two United crews reported jamming over Los Angeles inside an FAA test window. Rutgers sits between Route 18, Route 1, I-287 and the NJ Turnpike, the corridor where a truck driver's GPS jammer once disrupted a navigation system at Newark airport. Lost Time puts five cheap, listen-only GNSS interference monitors on campus windows and roofs for months. It logs every event, models when an interference event outlasts a site's holdover, and gives Verizon a ranked list of the C-band sites that should get network-delivered timing first, before the upper C-band adds more TDD spectrum.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) TDD needs phase sync: 3GPP TS 38.133 sets cell phase synchronization accuracy for NR TDD (3 us between cells), and ITU-T G.8271 defines the time-accuracy levels used for it (https://www.itu.int/rec/T-REC-G.8271; standard text not re-read this pass). (2) 2026 US GPS-loss events. Fox News (Aug 7, 2026) reports that on May 14 a King Air air ambulance from Roswell crashed approaching Ruidoso, NM, killing four. The crew had reported losing GPS while a scheduled military GPS-denial exercise was under way, with an FAA notice warning of disruption hundreds of miles around White Sands; controllers twice asked the military to stop, and three other aircraft reported GPS outages. The NTSB has not found a cause. The FAA tells pilots jamming is 'of greater and growing concern'. https://www.foxnews.com/politics/fatal-air-ambulance-crash-exposes-growing-gps-danger-facing-us-flights. AeroCorner (Sept 25, 2026): United UA1962 and UA1866 reported GPS jamming near Los Angeles at 07:36 and 08:15 UTC, inside FAA advisory CHLKCA GPS 26-07's nightly China Lake test window (Sept 20-25), whose zone reaches 346 nm at altitude. No official link was made. https://aerocorner.com/news/united-ua1962-ua1866-gps-jamming-los-angeles/ (3) New Jersey precedent: the FCC proposed a fine of about $32,000 against a driver whose GPS jammer, used on the highway by Newark airport, interfered with a navigation system being installed there (2013). https://insidegnss.com/fcc-campaign-goes-after-gps-and-cell-phone-jammers/ (4) Jammers in cars have hit carrier networks: a Florida commuter's cell jammer disrupted MetroPCS towers on his route and drew a $48,000 FCC fine (2014). https://baynews9.com/fl/tampa/news/2014/4/30/seffner_man_cell_tow (5) Detection is a known gap. GPS.gov says the US addresses interference through regulation, detection and enforcement and asks users to report it: https://www.gps.gov/node/39. A 2013 DHS briefing said the US 'lacks the capability to rapidly detect and geo-locate harmful jamming or spoofing of GPS services inside the United States': https://www.gps.gov/cgsic/meetings/2013/merrill.pdf (6) More TDD is coming: FCC 26-46 (adopted Jul 22, 2026) opens 3.98-4.14 GHz, with the auction scheduled for Apr 27, 2027. https://www.federalregister.gov/documents/2026/08/03/2026-15725 (7) 49 CFR 395.3: property-carrying drivers take 10 consecutive hours off duty, so a parked truck with a jammer left on can sit near a site for hours. https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-49/subtitle-B/chapter-III/subchapter-B/part-395/section-395.3 UNVERIFIED: Verizon's mix of GNSS and PTP timing at C-band sites, its holdover oscillators, and any documented US cell outage caused by GPS jamming (none found).
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon C-band n77 TDD (and any upper C-band it wins), GNSS-disciplined site clocks with holdover oscillators, IEEE 1588 PTP (ITU-T G.8275.1/.2 profiles) over Verizon fiber backhaul as the network-delivered alternative, and the GNSS-lock alarms in Verizon's network-operations data. Student monitors are u-blox receivers that report AGC, C/N0 and a jamming indicator, plus an RTL-SDR watching L1. They only listen. Each reports over its own ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M line ($1.50-$6/mo, verified), so the monitors keep reporting through campus network outages (Apr 27, Aug 7 and Oct 12, 2026). Roadmap: the same sensor network plus Rutgers' NJ Weather & Climate Network and coastal-ocean data could flag atmospheric-ducting mornings that cause TDD remote interference, a problem vendors already build for (Ericsson's remote interference management overview, https://www.ericsson.com/en/blog/2019/9/overview-of-remote-interference-management; Nokia patent US12463737B1, Nov 4, 2025).
- **Verizon money:** This moves capex (2026 guide $16.0-16.5B) and cost of services (interference investigations, drive tests, truck rolls), and it protects the C-band investment from Auction 107. Formula with labeled assumptions: value = N_sites x P_event x C_incident - N_upgraded x C_upgrade. Ranking sites by measured interference exposure lets Verizon upgrade the right few percent of sites to PTP or better holdover instead of running a blanket program. Every input is UNVERIFIED without Verizon data. Honest view: this is risk reduction with no public incident to price. A second-order product, 'GPS-free time' sold over Verizon fiber or 5G to critical sites (substation PMUs, 911 center clocks, the NJ supercomputer at SHI), is roadmap only.
- **Dated anchor:** May 14, 2026 New Mexico crash with GPS loss during a military jamming exercise (reported Aug 7, 2026); Sept 25, 2026 Los Angeles jamming reports inside FAA advisory CHLKCA GPS 26-07's window; FCC 26-46 (Jul 22, 2026; Federal Register Aug 3, 2026) schedules the upper C-band auction for Apr 27, 2027. The NJ highway-corridor interference log itself does not exist anywhere yet.
- **Primary data:** Months of continuous, timestamped GNSS-interference logs from 3-5 sites near Route 18 (College Ave, Busch, Livingston) and Route 1 (Cook/Douglass), with event duration, strength and rough bearing from multiple sensors, correlated with time of day and traffic counts. No public dataset of jamming frequency along New Jersey highway corridors near cell sites exists. We never transmit or jam.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): 0:00 the 2026 headlines (New Mexico, Los Angeles), then the Newark jammer story. 0:20 a truck passes on Route 18 and the live monitor spikes: 'L1 C/N0 -22 dB, 41 s' (if captured). 0:40 a holdover simulator shows a site clock drifting toward the TDD limit in a 10-hour 'parked truck' scenario, beside a PTP-fed site that stays flat. 1:05 the map of logged events next to public cell-site locations, ranked 'upgrade first'. 1:30 the tie-in: 'The upper C-band auction is Apr 27, 2027. Fix time before you add spectrum.' POSTER TABLE: a live receiver showing satellites and C/N0, a Faraday-bag demo of signal loss (no transmitter), and the event histogram.
- **Why unique:** Timing is invisible to app-minded teams and to LLM defaults. The chain from TDD to GPS to highway jammers and military tests is contrarian and physical, and network engineers recognize it at once. The data needs months of on-site monitoring that only a resident team can run.
- **Biggest risk:** The monitors may see few or no jamming events at campus distances from the highways, and without events the story collapses. Verizon probably already uses multi-GNSS antennas, PTP and good holdover, and a judge may say a passing truck does not matter. The 2026 events are aviation, not cellular, and no US cell outage from GPS jamming is documented. The money cannot be quantified without Verizon data.

## I10: Shared Fate: Rutgers' 'redundant' network links may cross the Raritan in the same place (lens contrarian, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services (enterprise resilience the customer can see), with Pillar 2 (one wireless leg instead of a second trench), self-score 44)

**One-liner:** Universities and hospitals pay for two carriers so that one fiber cut cannot take them down, but almost nobody checks whether both paths ride the same bridge, conduit or pole line. Rutgers' campuses are split by the Raritan River. Students catalog every physical crossing between New Brunswick and Piscataway and, with OIT, match the crossings to Rutgers' circuit routes and its 2026 outage history to report which redundancy is real. Verizon sells the fix: route-documented diverse fiber, plus 5G Business Internet as a path that shares no physical risk with the wired ones.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) Rutgers had three campus-wide IT outages in 2026. Oct 12: an unplanned network outage from 8:09 AM to 2:06 PM, escalated to an outside service provider, with no root cause published. https://it.rutgers.edu/alerts/2026/10/12/issues-with-network-and-access-to-it-services/. Aug 7-8: a power problem took down RUWireless Secure, wired networking at some locations and VPN overnight. https://it.rutgers.edu/alerts/2026/08/07/some-it-services-unavailable-due-to-power-outage/. Apr 27: a data-center power outage took down rutgers.edu, myRutgers, Wi-Fi, VPN and payroll most of the day. https://it.rutgers.edu/alerts/2026/04/27/issues-with-network-connectivity-and-it-services/ (2) Rutgers appointed a Chief Risk Officer to oversee Enterprise Risk Management (Oct 2026). https://www.dailytargum.com/article/u-apppoints-former-cornell-compliance-worker-as-chief-risk-officer-20261002 (3) Physical cuts are rising. In Aug 2026, vandalized Verizon fiber caused a day-long Southern California outage with phones in SOS: https://abc7.com/post/vandalized-fiber-lines-cause-verizon-service-outage-thousands-southern-california-customers/19653900/. Verizon offered a $25K reward and cited an industry figure of 6,297 network attacks in California in 2025: https://www.verizon.com/about/news/es/verizon-ofrece-recompensa-por-vandalismo-de-red (4) Rutgers already buys from two voice carriers: its 848-445 and 848-932 blocks are held by PAETEC ITEL (Windstream), and its legacy 732 blocks by Verizon NJ. https://www.localcallingguide.com/lca_prefix.php?npa=848&nxx=445 (5) More digging near campus conduits: on Oct 1, 2026 the Board of Governors approved hot-water piping replacement on Busch and Livingston. https://www.dailytargum.com/article/u-board-of-governors-create-real-estate-degree-program-new-professorships-approve-campus-repairs-20261009 UNVERIFIED: Rutgers' actual circuit routes, which carriers cross the river where, and whether any shared risk exists.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon fiber and Ethernet access with documented physically diverse routing (route maps), and Verizon 5G Business Internet or LTE Business Internet as a wireless access leg with no shared conduit or bridge, fed by different backhaul. A shared-risk-link-group (SRLG) model of the kind Verizon's transport planners use. Out-of-band health checks for each path ride a ThingSpace LTE-M line so that they survive the outage they measure.
- **Verizon money:** This moves Enterprise & Public Sector revenue ($13.5B, -4.8% in FY2025), specifically diverse access and wireless backup attach at universities and hospitals. Formula with labeled assumptions: revenue = A x (L x $B + D x $P) x 12. ASSUMED: A = 3 New Brunswick anchors (Rutgers, RWJUH, Saint Peter's); L = 10 critical sites each with a 5G backup line at $B = $150/mo; D = 1 diverse-route circuit each at a $P = $2,000/mo premium. That is about $130K/yr locally. Honest view: small at one campus. The value is a repeatable E&PS sales motion ('we show you your shared fate, then sell you the cure') for a segment whose revenue is shrinking.
- **Dated anchor:** Oct 12, 2026: a six-hour Rutgers network outage with no published root cause. Oct 1-2, 2026: a new Chief Risk Officer, and Board approval of excavation on Busch and Livingston. Aug 2026: Verizon fiber vandalism leaves phones in SOS. Apr 27 and Aug 7, 2026: power-driven Rutgers IT outages.
- **Primary data:** (1) A photo catalog of every Raritan crossing between New Brunswick and Piscataway (road and rail bridges, pipe and utility crossings), recording the telecom conduit and cable attached to each, shared only privately with OIT and the CRO. (2) With OIT, Rutgers' WAN and internet circuit list and the carriers' route documents, run through an SRLG analysis. (3) Traceroute and latency fingerprints from each campus to find hidden common paths. (4) A cause analysis of the Apr 27, Aug 7 and Oct 12, 2026 outages from public OIT alerts.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): 0:00 the Oct 12 OIT alert on screen. 0:15 students under a Raritan bridge, conduits highlighted (generic, no sensitive detail). 0:35 the SRLG graph: 'Path A and Path B both depend on crossing 3' (if found). 1:00 a simulated cut of that crossing: wired paths go red, and a 5G Business Internet unit on a rooftop keeps a critical service up. 1:30 the CRO-style risk-register entry and the Verizon offer. POSTER TABLE: the crossing catalog in aggregate, a live dual-path ping board (wired versus Verizon 5G), and the SRLG model.
- **Why unique:** Diversity audits are a boring line item no app team touches, and a river is a uniquely physical Rutgers fact. It turns Rutgers' own 2026 outages and new CRO into a sales motion for a shrinking Verizon segment.
- **Biggest risk:** OIT may refuse to share routes, which are security-sensitive, and then the analysis is speculation. Underground crossings are invisible. The result may show Rutgers is fine. Publishing crossing details could create a security problem, so only aggregates go public. Verizon already knows its own routes. The money is small, and the idea is close to other '5G as the backup access path' pitches.

## I11: Remedy Router: fix the place, not the phone (lens cost_lines, pillar Pillar 2: Cost & Sustainability Transformation (retention spend and equipment loss). Pillar 1 is secondary: an AI triage tool for Verizon's care agent., self-score 66)

**One-liner:** When a customer says 'no signal at home', a 60-second consented phone scan plus a decision engine tells the save agent which fix to offer: turn on Wi-Fi Calling ($0), ship the $175 LTE Network Extender, sell Verizon One home internet (bundles churn 40% less), escalate to network planning, or, only when the phone really is the problem, give a handset. Rutgers students test this against what Verizon stores, newly franchised stores and AI care offer today.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) Schulman, Q1 2026 call (Apr 27 2026): 'previously, what we would have done is send them a free handset ... So we just spent like $1,000 and did not solve the customer's issues. If we had listened and sent a femtocell ... we could have done that at one-third the cost' and 'The era of just the free handset, that's gone' (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-06/vz_1q26_transcript_042726rm.pdf). (2) FY2025 wireless equipment revenue $25,470M vs cost $28,976M, about -$3.5B (derived; https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/732712/000073271226000003/a2025q4exhibit99.htm). (3) Q2 2026: promotional cost of retention down about 17% YoY and upgrade volumes down nearly 27% (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-08/vz_2q2026_transcript_072426rm.pdf). The Q2 10-Q attributes a $1.1B drop in consumer equipment cost to a 'strategic decrease in upgrades' (https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/732712/000073271226000046/vz-20260630.htm). (4) Bundled mobility+broadband customers show 'a 40% reduction in churn versus stand-alone Mobility', and 'every single basis point is 90,000 net adds' (Jan 30 2026, https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-02/vz_4q25_transcript_013026r.pdf). (5) The remedy catalog has gaps. Verizon lists its Samsung LTE Network Extenders as 'no longer supported' (https://www.verizon.com/support/network-extender-faqs/). The current Verizon LTE Network Extender is $174.99 and rated 3.2/5 on 1.7K reviews (fetched 2026-10-21, https://www.verizon.com/products/verizon-lte-network-extender/). (6) Channel change: 274 company stores moved to franchisees on Aug 16 2026 (https://www.fox29.com/news/verizon-sell-nearly-300-stores-cut-500-jobs-restructuring). Google's Gemini Enterprise for CX 'already handles the majority of Verizon's monthly inbound consumer calls and chats' (Aug 2026, https://pureai.com/blogs/the-pure-ai-blog/2026/08/verizon-expands-google-cloud-partnership.aspx).
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon LTE Network Extender (a femtocell on the customer's broadband, up to 16 users). Wi-Fi Calling (IMS voice over ePDG). Verizon Home Internet 5G/LTE fixed wireless and the Verizon One mobile+home bundle ($70, Jun 16 2026). The scan checks whether the device supports C-band n77 and 5G UW. It reads RSRP/RSRQ/SINR, serving band and Wi-Fi Calling state from Android TelephonyManager, comparing the interior with the window. Two outputs go back into Verizon: (a) a tool call for the Gemini-based care agent and the store tablet; (b) anonymized clusters of weak places, sent to network planning as candidate small-cell or tilt work.
- **Verizon money:** Money lines: promotional cost of retention (not disclosed in dollars) and equipment sold below cost (about -$3.5B in FY2025). Using Schulman's own ratio, each save converted from a handset (about $1,000) to a place fix (about one-third) saves about $667. So 10,000 converted saves is about $6.7M (labeled assumption on volume; Verizon does not disclose coverage-save counts). The Verizon One/FWA remedy turns a save cost into new broadband revenue and puts the customer in the segment with 40% lower churn. Honest size: tens of millions of dollars a year nationally if 50-100K saves a year are redirected. It is not billions. The pilot proves the triage and the field gap; Verizon supplies the volumes.
- **Dated anchor:** Jul 24 2026 Q2 call: CoR down 17%, upgrades down 27%, 'we are separating phone subsidies from our Wireless pricing'. Jul 31 2026 10-Q: 'strategic decrease in upgrades'. Jun 16 2026: Verizon One $70 mobile+home. Aug 16 2026: 274 stores franchised. Aug 2026: Gemini handles most inbound care. Oct 21 2026 (fetched): the Network Extender sells for $174.99 and is rated 3.2/5, while the Samsung extenders are 'no longer supported'. Root anchor: Schulman's Apr 27 2026 femtocell anecdote.
- **Primary data:** (1) Remedy audit. About 20 honest scripted contacts, made by team members who really have weak indoor signal where they live, across corporate and newly franchised stores within about 15 miles of New Brunswick, Verizon chat and 611 phone care. We record the first remedy offered (handset, extender, Wi-Fi Calling, home internet or none), the time taken and the price. No recordings, notes only, and no offer is accepted. (2) In-room RF census. 40-60 dorm rooms and off-campus rooms (Busch, Livingston, College Ave, Cook/Douglass, 5th Ward rentals), measuring interior vs window RSRP/RSRQ/SINR, band, Wi-Fi Calling state and device C-band capability. This gives the root-cause split: configuration, place, device, or outdoor network. (3) 5-8 before/after remedy trials: Wi-Fi Calling switched on, 2 extenders (about $350) and 1 FWA window unit, measuring call success and throughput. All data comes from consenting volunteers' own phones. There is no CPNI.
- **Demo:** Video (2:00). 0:00 Schulman's quote on screen. 0:15 a basement room shows 1 bar, and a re-enacted counter offers a new $1,000 phone. 0:35 the Save Scan runs for 60 s: 'indoor loss 19 dB, window SINR good, Wi-Fi Calling OFF, phone C-band capable.
