- **Primary data:** 1) 14-day stationary-fingerprint study: 12 ThingSpace self-serve LTE modules ($1.50-$6/month each) in machine rooms, basements, dorms and labs across Busch, Livingston, College Ave and Cook/Douglass, logging serving cell, PCI, timing advance and RSRP every 10 minutes.
- It measures how often a box that never moves appears to move (the false-alarm rate) and the smallest move detectable: a floor, a building, or 2.9 km from Busch to Livingston.
- It finishes with a deliberate move test. No public dataset exists for Verizon fixed IoT endpoints.
2) Label census: photograph the posted location ID at every IBC 1009.8 refuge or elevator-landing device in 30 buildings and compare it against the Rutgers building list and the building's posted address.
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT: a box on a cart, registered 'Hill Center, Busch'. We drive it to Livingston, and by the next fingerprint window the console reads 'moved about 2.9 km; registered 911 location at risk; owner notified'. A second screen shows the conversion gate: 'old ALI: HILL CTR ELEV 2 / new registration: university HQ street address -> BLOCKED until fixed'.
POSTER TABLE: a map of the 12 sensors with 14 days of fingerprint stability, plus a live module visitors can carry across the hall to watch the drift score rise.
- **Why unique:** - Verizon's own process makes the customer the only source of truth, and the network is the one party that can independently see where a fixed box lives.
- The stationary-fingerprint dataset is primary data.
- Distinct from ECRC Zero (PBX extension location data) and from our explored Wi-Fi Calling address ideas (Move-In 911, RightRoom), because it targets fixed cellular lifeline boxes and the copper-to-wireless conversion record.
- **Biggest risk:** - Cell-level fingerprints cannot resolve floors or cars, so it catches moves and bulk mis-registrations, not elevator car numbers (the champion's label check does that).
- How a BPC100's 911 call actually carries location (registered address vs network location) is UNVERIFIED.
- Verizon may already flag moved boxes.
- Overlaps the ECRC Zero family.
- Small dollars.

## I45: NOTHING BROKE: lifelines that go silent because a prepaid SIM, a vendor account or a certificate ran out (lens silent_failures, pillar Pillar 1, AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency: automated detection of administrative failure. Pillar 3 (trust for enterprise IoT customers) is secondary., self-score 59)

**One-liner:** Some lifelines die with no physical fault:
- a two-year prepaid SIM bundled in a retrofit kit expires;
- a vendor moves monitoring to a new portal and units without an account 'cannot be monitored or tested';
- a rival carrier switches off 2G;
- a campus Wi-Fi network is retired for new certificates.
Only the carrier can see a lifeline device knocking at a closed door. NOTHING BROKE adds a 'lifeline' flag to Verizon IoT and voice lines: no silent suspension, same-night owner alerts on attach rejects, a plan-expiry radar, and one-tap continuity onto a Verizon eSIM.

- **Problem evidence:** 1) Talkaphone's VoLTE blue-light retrofit kit (announced Feb 12 2025) bundles a 'TWO-YEAR PREPAID AT&T SIM CARD WITH VOICE OVER LTE (VOLTE) SERVICE'. Its cloud supervision covers only gateway online/offline and SIM removal. Inference: the first kits reach the end of prepaid service around Feb 2027. https://www.talkaphone.com/products/blue-light-phones/retrofit-kit/
2) RATH SmartView 1 elevator visual systems: final orders Feb 1 2025, and monitoring moved to smartviewhub.com on Jan 27 2025. Devices without a Hub account cannot be monitored or tested. https://www.avire-global.com/en-us/products/elevator-two-way-visual-communication/
3) T-Mobile shut its 2G GSM network on Aug 3 2026 (Tech Times, Jul 7 2026; secondary source). https://www.techtimes.com/articles/319869/20260707/t-mobile-2g-gsm-ends-august-3-iot-operators-face-two-migration-waves-not-one.htm
4) Rutgers' RUWireless Secure turns off on Jan 5 2027 as 'part of a broader effort to update the digital certificate infrastructure'. Headless devices that cannot re-enroll go quiet. https://it.rutgers.edu/2026/09/22/wi-fi-at-rutgers-is-moving-to-eduroam/
5) Rutgers' blue lights are 'managed and monitored by an external vendor, Knightscope', with daily health reports. Their carrier and plan are UNVERIFIED. https://it.rutgers.edu/rutgers-next-gen-network/next-gen-network-enhancements/
6) Verizon's ThingSpace Terms of Service (Aug 2024) limit plans to IoT use, have no life-safety carve-out, and use is 'at your own risk'. https://thingspace.verizon.com/legal/terms-of-service.html
7) Precedent: at the 3G sunset, customers who did not respond lost signal despite about $292M of ADT conversions, and about 40% of the roughly 6M stranded alarm radios were on Verizon (AICC 2021).
https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1703056/000170305623000046/adt-20221231.htm ; https://tma.us/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AICC-Petition-for-Emergency-Relief-5-10-21-FINAL.pdf
- **Verizon tech:** - ThingSpace Connectivity Management API: activate and suspend, connection history, reachability status, callback URLs. https://thingspace.verizon.com/documentation/apis/connectivity-management.html
- Network attach and registration reject logs (MME/AMF cause codes): the device is alive but the network is refusing it.
- Verizon IoT Network Continuity eSIM (flyer created Oct 8 2026) to move a stranded device onto a Verizon profile. https://www.verizon.com/business/resources/flyers/iot-network-continuity.pdf
- LTE-M 'lifeboat' modules for Wi-Fi-only alarm devices stranded by a campus cutover.
The flag drives four behaviours:
(a) billing, inactivity or fraud suspension of a flagged line needs confirmation from the premises owner, not only the reseller;
(b) repeated rejects trigger a same-night owner alert;
(c) expiry radar for flagged plans;
(d) one-tap continuity.
- **Verizon money:** E&PS IoT growth and win-back:
- every competitor lifeline SIM that expires (AT&T-only retrofit kits) or is stranded (T-Mobile 2G) is a switching moment;
- 'Verizon never silently suspends a lifeline' is a promise no rival makes.
It also avoids the headline of a Verizon suspension silencing an elevator during a forced migration.
Price anchors: ThingSpace $1.50-$60/month; One Talk from $20/line.
CFO formula, inputs are ASSUMPTIONS: lifeline lines won back or kept x ARPU x 12. For example, 20,000 lines x $10 x 12 = $2.4M/yr. Honest: small dollars; strategic trust in E&PS (FY2025 revenue $13.5B, down 4.8%).
- **Dated anchor:** - T-Mobile 2G off Aug 3 2026.
- Verizon IoT Network Continuity flyer, Oct 8 2026.
- RUWireless Secure shut-off announced Sept 22 2026, effective Jan 5 2027.
- FCC 26-19 effective Oct 15 2026: carriers no longer show replacement interoperability.
- The first two-year Talkaphone prepaid SIMs expire from about Feb 2027 (inference).
- **Primary data:** Lifeline SIM and subscription census, the first for any campus. For every cellular or cloud-monitored lifeline (blue lights, elevator cellular phones, StarLink communicators, AED and refuge monitors), record:
- the carrier, read from the ICCID prefix (89148 Verizon, 890141 AT&T, 890126 T-Mobile);
- the plan owner (Rutgers, vendor or contractor);
- the renewal or expiry date;
- the vendor-cloud dependency;
- who is told if it lapses.
Sources: Facilities, plus OPRA for the Knightscope and elevator-maintenance contracts with term dates. We also ask OIT how many non-personal devices are still on RUWireless Secure.
Output: 'N Rutgers lifelines whose only failure mode is an invoice or a certificate.'
- **Demo:** VIDEO BEAT: 'Nothing broke. An invoice did.' A blue-light stand-in (an LTE-M dev board on our own ThingSpace self-serve line) is running. On camera we suspend the line through the Connectivity Management API. The vendor-style dashboard just says 'offline', and nobody is told. Our console says 'Lifeline BL-14: plan suspended 02:13 by billing; device alive, network refusing (attach reject); owner notified; one-tap restore', and restore brings it back.
POSTER TABLE: visitors flip cards (prepaid expiry, vendor portal lapse, 2G shutdown, Wi-Fi certificate change) and watch which alert fires and who receives it.
- **Why unique:** - Failures with no physical cause are in nobody's test plan; line checks, acoustic tests and battery telemetry all pass until the account dies.
- Only the carrier sees a device knocking.
- The Rutgers SIM and subscription census is primary data.
- Distinct from Re-Select (winning IoT devices back after T-Mobile network changes) because the trigger is administrative death and the product is a no-silent-suspend policy plus owner notification. Not on the AVOID lists.
- **Biggest risk:** - Verizon may see 'no suspend without owner confirmation' as credit risk.
- Most lifeline SIMs belong to vendors, so the premises owner is not Verizon's customer and owner notification needs vendor contract terms.
- The census needs Facilities access.
- The Talkaphone expiry date is our inference, and Knightscope's carrier is unknown.
- Revenue is small.

## I46: Plug-and-Prove: the copper-replacement box tests itself and prints the receipt, so the truck goes only to failures (lens tactile_demo, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (zero-touch install verification replaces a technician visit). Pillar 3 (customer experience at a forced migration) is secondary., self-score 76)

**One-liner:** Voice Connect, Verizon's home-phone replacement where copper goes away, is the same Inseego BPC100 sold as Business Phone Connect. Verizon sends a technician to install it, because 'compatibility is determined by your testing of your alarm and/or medical device at time of install.' Plug-and-Prove has the box test itself. The customer plugs in the phone, alarm panel, medical-alert base and fax and presses one button. About 90 seconds later a receipt prints: what works on Verizon's network, what does not, and the one fix for each. Verizon already mails BPC100s for customers to swap in themselves, so Plug-and-Prove extends that self-install path to first-time copper migrations.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED (fetched 2026-10-21):
(1) Verizon Voice Connect support page: 'a highly reliable home phone service by utilizing a wireless signal received by a professionally installed device', and 'calls to E911 and any alarm or medical devices previously connected and tested with the device'. https://www.verizon.com/support/consumer/homephone/voice-connect
(2) Voice Connect 2nd Gen user guide. Its footer reads 'BPC100 User Guide', (c) 2022. Quotes: 'Compatibility is determined by your testing of your alarm and/or medical device at time of install.' 'Failure to verify may result in your alarm not working.' 'Verizon is not responsible for unsuccessful alarm responses or medical device relays.' https://www.verizon.com/content/dam/verizon/support/consumer/documents/phone/voice-connect-user-guide-2nd-gen.pdf
(3) Self-install precedent. Verizon's T2050-to-BPC100 swap guide: 'Verizon is upgrading your voice equipment to our latest Voice Connect device... swapping out the device is easy.' The customer activates by calling 1-800-Verizon and is told to 'make a test call'. The words alarm and medical appear zero times in that guide. https://www.verizon.com/content/dam/verizon/support/consumer/documents/phone/voice-connect-upgrade-instructions.pdf
(4) ABC11, Aug 14 2026: VoiceConnect 'is installed and serviced by the same reliable technicians who work on our networks today'. Customer options are Fiber, VoiceConnect and Wireless Broadband. https://abc11.com/post/landlines-nc-man-concerned-frontier-verizon-company-warned-longtime-traditional-phone-service-could-discontinued/19678719/
(5) FCC 26-19 rules took effect Oct 15 2026. They remove the 63.602 adequate-replacement test, which covered interoperability with key applications. They also remove the 63.71(a)(6) notice items 'description of any security responsibilities' and 'A list of the steps the customer may take to ensure safe use of the replacement service'. Source: a diff of eCFR 63.71 as of 2026-10-14 against 2026-10-19 (https://www.ecfr.gov/api/versioner/v1/full/2026-10-14/title-47.xml?part=63&section=63.71), plus https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf
(6) Scale. The 2026 copper notices cover 1,763 unique wire-center CLLIs in 21 states (our parse of the Verizon and Frontier notices). NJ notice 2026-03-A-NJ covers 62 wire centers, including Piscataway, on or after Dec 10 2026: https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/verizon-new-jersey-copper-retirement-notice-2026-0908.pdf. DA 26-891 (WC 26-228, B1 and key-system lines, NJ included) is authorized on or after Oct 23 2026: https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-891A1.pdf
(7) Truck-roll friction. AICC says a migration truck roll takes up to 3 h and about 25% of appointments get no access: https://tma.us/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AICC-Petition-for-Emergency-Relief-5-10-21-FINAL.pdf. Verizon's copper savings came partly from 'a reduction in technician dispatches' (Investor Day 2022): https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2022-03/Investor-Day-2022-Transcript.pdf. An industry estimate puts a truck roll at $150-600 (Asentria, secondary): https://www.asentria.com/?p=3227
(8) Verizon's 2014 Voice Link record: fax machines, credit card machines, some medical alert devices and some alarm systems 'may not be compatible'. A Public Knowledge survey in the same record found 24% keep a landline for a medical alert device and 17% for home security. FCC 14-185: https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-14-185A1.pdf
- **Verizon tech:** Voice Connect / Business Phone Connect, the Inseego BPC100: Cat-4 LTE, HD VoLTE, an RJ11 port and an RJ31 alarm port, and 'Verizon MDM for remote management'. It runs on Verizon's production VoLTE/IMS core.
- PROVE button: a ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M dongle ($1.50/mo plan). It runs the test plan and reports results, plus RSRP/RSRQ/SINR at the placement spot, through the Connectivity Management callbacks and Device Diagnostics APIs. It stays independent of the line under test.
- Test endpoints on real numbers: an Asterisk + SpanDSP responder that measures voice round-trip and DTMF integrity, Contact ID kiss-off (Asterisk AlarmReceiver), a fax T.30 handshake and pages at 9600 bps, and TTY Baudot echo.
- Battery-presence check: AC pulled for 10 s while dial tone is watched.
- Alarm-number registration check: did ##21 take?
- Production path: the same test plan as BPC100 firmware/MDM, with results written to the order record.
- **Verizon money:** Cost of services ($27.8B FY2025): install dispatches avoided on copper-to-wireless migrations. The CFO formula uses labeled assumptions only:
savings = migrated voice lines x share that pass the self-proof x cost per avoided roll.
- Illustration only: 100,000 lines x 60% x $250 = $15M one-time. A real number needs Verizon's migration volumes and dispatch cost, neither of which is disclosed.
- AICC's 25% no-access rate means some booked rolls are wasted outright.
- Secondary lever: faster exit. The 2022 program saved about $180M/yr from 36 COs, roughly $5M per CO per year as a proxy, so each month earlier at a CO is worth about $0.4M. That is Verizon's saving, not ours to claim.
- Tertiary: retention at a forced migration (customers who would otherwise pick cable VoIP). Not credited.
Honest size: tens of millions one-time at national scale, near zero at Rutgers. This is a cost-line idea, not new revenue.
- **Dated anchor:** - Oct 15 2026: FCC 26-19 rules take effect, deleting the adequate-replacement test and the 'steps to ensure safe use' customer notice (eCFR diff 2026-10-14 vs 2026-10-19).
- Aug 14 2026: Verizon publicly names VoiceConnect as its technician-installed non-fiber replacement (ABC11).
- Sept 25 2026: the FCC pulls Verizon's 9-state legacy-voice exit (WC 26-227, includes NJ) from auto-grant (DA 26-1038).
- Oct 23 2026: authorized date for the B1/key-system exit (DA 26-891).
- Non-obvious and verified: the Voice Connect 2nd Gen guide is literally the 'BPC100 User Guide', so a $129.99 retail device lets students test Verizon's exact residential replacement.
- **Primary data:** (1) Self-install study with the landline-keeping demographic. Recruit 20+ older adults through Rutgers' Osher Lifelong Learning Institute (OLLI-RU) or the retiree association (recruitment UNCONFIRMED; needs an IRB exempt determination). Each sets up a BPC100 with and without Plug-and-Prove. Measure completion rate, time, and errors, for example plugging the phone into the yellow alarm port, which the guide warns 'could stop the device from working'. Count which failures truly need a technician.
(2) Device-compatibility bench on Verizon's production network. 8-12 secondhand alarm panels, 3-4 landline medical-alert bases, 2 fax machines and 1 TTY, each run 50-100 times over the BPC100 and over a copper line simulator. This gives the first public pass-rate matrix behind Verizon's own words 'most' (Voice Connect page) and 'many' (verizon.com/copper).
(3) A consented survey of Rutgers staff who live in the 62 NJ wire centers: what is plugged into their home line, and whether the main handset is cordless.
No other team has a Verizon replacement box, an older-adult test panel, or a measured matrix.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (20-s beat):
- Hold up a BPC100: 'This is the box Verizon puts in homes when copper goes. Today a technician drives out to plug it in.'
- Hands plug a 1990s phone, an alarm keypad and a medical-alert base into it and press PROVE.
- Cut to a thermal printer. Receipt lines scroll: VOICE ok, ALARM Contact ID kiss-off 5.8 s ok, ALARM NUMBER registered ok, MEDICAL ALERT ok, FAX 9600 ok, SIGNAL here good.
- One red line: 'BATTERY: none installed. 3 AAs in the mail.' Stamp: TRUCK NOT NEEDED.
- Close on 2 numbers: the older-adult study (x of 20 finished alone) and the bench (y% of devices pass).
POSTER TABLE: judges plug in and press the button themselves, then take the receipt home. A 'break it' card invites them to pull the batteries, use the alarm port for the phone, or unplug the antenna, and the receipt catches each one.
BOM under $800:
- BPC100: $129.99
- Raspberry Pi with Asterisk/SpanDSP and an HT813 FXS/FXO: about $150
- LTE-M dongle: about $60
- Receipt printer: about $50
- Used alarm panel, medical-alert base and fax: about $200
Build plan for the Nov 8 video: voice, alarm and battery checks only. Fax, medical and TTY follow by Feb 3.
- **Why unique:** - Built on a Verizon-specific chain other teams will not assemble: Voice Connect = BPC100; installed by technicians; compatibility testing pushed onto the customer at install; a mailed self-swap program that already exists. Add rule deletions that took effect six days before today.
- It attacks a cost line the GN&T judges own, technician dispatches, with an object they can hold and a receipt they keep.
- Not Last Copper: this is homes and small businesses at the moment of install, with no elevators, in-car acoustics or campus lifelines.
- It composes with Dark Hours (battery), Basement Truth (signal at the spot) and Kiss-Off Check (alarm over time) into one Verizon cutover kit.
- **Biggest risk:** - Verizon may keep the technician visit for 911-address accuracy and liability, or may already run remote activation checks, which shrinks the dispatch savings.
- Overlaps a peer concept ('Still Works?', a pre-migration device check).
- Building fax, alarm and medical responders plus an older-adult study in 18 days is tight. Fallback for Nov 8: voice, alarm and battery only.
- Medical-alert test calls must be pre-arranged with the monitoring company. Never dial 911.
- A BPC100 line may need a Verizon Business account (use a faculty or department account).
- IRB timing.

## I47: Dark Hours: the copper replacement runs on 3 AA batteries, and the rules that made anyone say so were deleted (lens tactile_demo, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services to Enhance Customer Experience (an outage-aware home phone). Pillar 1 automation (outage-to-household triage) is secondary., self-score 73)

**One-liner:** Copper phones take power from the central office and kept working through New Jersey blackouts. Verizon's replacement, Voice Connect (the BPC100), runs 'up to 3 talk hours, or up to 23 standby hours' on 3 alkaline AAs. The guide says those batteries 'should also be replaced every six months', and it notes that 'a phone that does not have an electrical base station' is required. The FCC's backup-power rule ended Sept 1 2025, and since Oct 15 2026 discontinuance notices no longer have to say the replacement 'may not provide line power'. Dark Hours measures how long the boxes really last. When a blackout starts, it tells Verizon and a consented family contact how long each lifeline home has left, and it mails fresh lithium cells to those homes before the next storm.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED:
(1) Voice Connect 2nd Gen ('BPC100 User Guide'):
- 'your Voice Connect service will operate on battery power for up to 3 talk hours, or up to 23 standby hours. You will need to use a phone that does not have an electrical base station to make calls. When the battery runs out it will not be possible to make or receive calls, including calls to 911.'
- 'Batteries should also be replaced every six months or after a power outage.'
- 'The batteries in your Voice Connect device do not power your alarm panel or any phones connected to your Voice Connect device.'
https://www.verizon.com/content/dam/verizon/support/consumer/documents/phone/voice-connect-user-guide-2nd-gen.pdf
(2) Support page: 'If the device powers off after you remove AC power, then the batteries are either dead or not installed', and 'We highly recommend you insert batteries'. https://www.verizon.com/support/consumer/homephone/voice-connect
(3) The FCC 'Delete, Delete, Delete' Direct Final Rule, 91 FR 1400 (Jan 14 2026), effective Mar 16 2026: '47 CFR 9.20, has sunset by operation of law and ceased to be effective on September 1, 2025' and 'Remove and reserve § 9.20.' https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-14/pdf/2026-00612.pdf. Section 9.20 had required providers of non-line-powered residential fixed voice, 'including fixed applications of wireless service', to offer 8-hour and 24-hour backup options and to disclose expected duration and self-testing instructions at sale and annually. The text is in eCFR as of 2026-01-13 and is [Reserved] from 2026-03-16: https://www.ecfr.gov/api/versioner/v1/full/2026-01-13/title-47.xml?part=9&section=9.20
(4) FCC 26-19, effective Oct 15 2026. 63.71(a)(6)(i) 'A statement that any service offered in place of the service being discontinued... may not provide line power' and (ii) the backup-power disclosure appear in eCFR 2026-10-14 and are gone in 2026-10-19. https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf
(5) Early July 2026 storms: 'Nearly 140,000 NJ customers still lack power', Middlesex (Edison, Middlesex Borough) hit, and JCP&L 'could not give estimated restoration times' the next morning. NJ101.5, updated Jul 4 2026: https://nj1015.com/new-jersey-storm-power-outages/
(6) Verizon's copper FAQ promises battery backup 'using commercially available replaceable batteries' and gives no runtime. https://www.verizon.com/copper/
(7) 24% of landline keepers keep it for a medical alert device (Public Knowledge survey in FCC 14-185): https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-14-185A1.pdf
(8) The FCC pulled Verizon's 9-state legacy-voice exit (NJ included) from auto-grant (DA 26-1038, Sept 25 2026): https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1038A1.pdf
- **Verizon tech:** Production path: Voice Connect/BPC100 AC-loss and battery state through Verizon MDM (whether Verizon reads this today is UNVERIFIED), joined to Verizon's outage operations and generator-dispatch planning.
Prototype, which runs on Verizon production today:
- A ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M 'power sentinel' ($1.50/mo; Connectivity Management callbacks plus SMS) wired to the BPC100's AA tray through an INA219 sensor and to the outlet.
- It reports mains-loss, battery voltage and current draw, and predicts minutes left with a model fitted on our bench.
- Verizon SMS goes to a consented family contact, with call-forwarding to the customer's Verizon mobile as the one-tap fix.
- Utility outage polygons (PSE&G/JCP&L) are joined per wire center, so Verizon's network team sees how many battery-dependent lifeline homes sit behind each dark sector, an input to where portable generators go first.
- **Verizon money:** Mostly retention and risk, with small direct money.
Costs: 3 lithium AAs at about $5-8 per opted-in lifeline home per year (assumption).
Value levers:
(1) It de-risks the copper exit. Verizon's copper program saved about $180M/yr from 36 COs (2022), and the 9-state voice exit has been paused at the FCC since Sept 25. Evidence that battery-dependent homes are protected answers that class of objection. We do NOT claim it unblocks the docket.
(2) Cost of services: fewer post-storm care calls and 'my phone is dead' dispatches.
(3) Consumer/Business voice retention of older households at a forced migration.
Honest size: low single-digit $M per year directly. The indirect copper-exit value is larger but is not ours to claim.
- **Dated anchor:** - Oct 15 2026: FCC 26-19 deletes the 'may not provide line power' and backup-power notice from 63.71(a)(6) (eCFR diff, verified).
- Mar 16 2026: 'Delete, Delete, Delete' removes 47 CFR 9.20 from the CFR (sunset Sept 1 2025; 91 FR 1400).
- Early July 2026: NJ storms leave about 140,000 customers out the next day.
- Aug 14 2026: Verizon names VoiceConnect as the non-fiber replacement.
These are two power protections removed within 13 months, one of them six days before today.
- **Primary data:** (1) Battery-truth bench, the first independent check of '3 h talk / 23 h standby'. The BPC100 is run with fresh alkaline, 6-month-aged alkaline and lithium AAs, at 22 C and at 5 C (a mini-fridge stands in for a January basement), under three loads: idle, one 10-minute call a day, and alarm dial-outs.
(2) NJ outage-duration distribution for Middlesex County, compiled from NJ BPU major-event filings and utility reports, giving the share of outage customer-hours beyond 3 h and beyond 23 h. (Availability is to be confirmed.)
(3) A consented survey of older adults (OLLI-RU, UNCONFIRMED) and of Rutgers staff in the 62 NJ wire centers. Questions: is the only handset cordless (zero minutes in a blackout whatever the AAs), are batteries even installed (a pull-the-plug self-check card), and does a medical alert ride the line?
- **Demo:** VIDEO (20-s beat):
- Two identical 1990s phones. Left: a copper line simulator powered by a 48 V 'central office' battery. Right: Verizon's BPC100.
- A hand flips a power strip labeled STORM. Left: dial tone stays. Right: the LED blinks blue.
- On screen, a ThingSpace map of Piscataway lights one home and starts a countdown: 'talk 2:59:51 rated; measured at 5 C: x'.
- The judge's phone buzzes: 'Mom's home phone is on battery. Fresh batteries are on the way; tap to forward her calls to her mobile.'
- Time-lapse: the right phone goes dead. Caption: '...and 911 with it.'
- A second shot: a cordless phone dies the instant the power goes. Caption: 'x% of the older adults we surveyed have only this.'
POSTER TABLE: the judge flips the switch. A second unit loaded with 6-month-old AAs dies early on a sped-up clock. The judge's phone receives the alert within about 10 s.
BOM under $600: 2 BPC100s, a 2-line simulator, an LTE-M sentinel with INA219, AA stock and a mini-fridge.
- **Why unique:** - Two federal power protections were deleted in 13 months, one six days before today. Teams relying on LLM defaults will cite 47 CFR 9.20 as current law.
- The device is Verizon's own.
- The demo is a blackout you can watch.
- The cordless-phone survey statistic is primary data nobody publishes.
- Distinct from Last Copper (no elevators, no acoustic test) and from our 911-location ideas.
- **Biggest risk:** - Verizon value is mostly risk avoided, not revenue. Network-planning judges may rate the business case thin.
- Verizon MDM may already see AC/battery state. If so, our contribution narrows to measurement, the prioritization model and the battery mailer.
- A judge may answer 'customers can buy a UPS'.
- Flagging lifeline homes must be strictly opt-in to avoid CPNI misuse after the upheld $46.9M fine.
- Utility outage data may be too coarse to join per wire center.

## I48: Basement Truth: the FCC's adequacy test is an outdoor map at 1.5 m; the phone jack is in the basement (lens tactile_demo, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (a predictive pre-qualification model for network transformation). Pillar 2 (cost: failed installs and repeat dispatches) is secondary., self-score 71)

**One-liner:** Since Oct 15 2026, a carrier can retire copper voice automatically if it certifies 5/1 Mbps mobile service 'consistent with' the broadband-map parameters, and those maps 'must depict outdoor coverage'. Verizon's own Voice Connect page warns that moving the box 'could impact the 911 capabilities and signal strength'. Basement Truth measures Verizon's real signal where the copper actually ends (basements, utility rooms, back offices) in a retiring wire center and learns indoor loss by building type. It gives Verizon an address-level list of homes that need an external antenna, a Network Extender, 5G Home or fiber before the cut, and of homes that can safely self-install.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED:
(1) 47 CFR 63.71(f)(2)(ii), in eCFR as of 2026-10-19 and effective Oct 15 2026 under FCC 26-19: a technology-transition discontinuance 'may be automatically granted only if the applicant certifies that in every location... at least one of the following... (ii) A facilities-based mobile wireless service operating at speeds of at least 5 Mbps download and 1 Mbps upload, consistent with the coverage parameters set forth in § 1.7004(c)(3)'. https://www.ecfr.gov/api/versioner/v1/full/2026-10-19/title-47.xml?part=63&section=63.71
(2) 47 CFR 1.7004(c)(5): 'Propagation maps submitted by providers must depict outdoor coverage, to include both on-street or pedestrian stationary usage, and in-vehicle mobile usage'. (c)(3)(v) puts RSRP 'at the industry standard of 1.5 meters above ground level'. https://www.ecfr.gov/api/versioner/v1/full/2026-10-19/title-47.xml?part=1&section=1.7004
(3) FCC 26-19 accepted mobile coverage as an adequate replacement and declined extra verification: https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-26-19A1.pdf
(4) Verizon: 'Our wireless network covers 99% of the U.S. population' (https://www.verizon.com/copper/). On Voice Connect: 'Moving the base device could impact the 911 capabilities and signal strength' (https://www.verizon.com/support/consumer/homephone/voice-connect).
(5) MarketSpark, Verizon's life-safety partner: after a cellular POTS migration 'devices consistently showed full signal bars, yet... Fax transmissions are failing intermittently' because of RSRQ (https://www.marketspark.com/understanding-cellular-signal-quality/). Its FAQ says LTE POTS is not plug-and-play and may need relocation or antennas (https://www.marketspark.com/?p=2760).
(6) UNC-Chapel Hill lists 'cell signal strength and reliability' as the limitation of its cellular elevator phones (https://doa.nc.gov/media/15758/open). NAPCO sells 100-ft external antennas and picks the best of 3 carriers at power-up (https://www.napcosecurity.com/media/pdfs/A893-StarLink-MaxFire3.pdf).
(7) Schulman, Q1'26 call: a $1,000 free phone did not fix a home-coverage complaint, whereas a femtocell would have cost 'one-third the cost' (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-06/vz_1q26_transcript_042726rm.pdf).
(8) NJ notice 2026-03-A-NJ: 62 wire centers including Piscataway (PSWYNJPI), on or after Dec 10 2026.
UNVERIFIED: which Rutgers buildings PSWYNJPI serves (NXX does not prove the wire center).
- **Verizon tech:** Measurement and prediction stack:
- ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M probes ($1.50/mo) log RSRP/RSRQ/SINR and coverage-enhancement level by AT command every minute for 24 h, with attach/detach history pulled from the Connectivity Management API and Device Diagnostics.
- A BPC100 checks VoLTE registration and places scheduled test calls to a One Talk number; together these are the real Voice Connect criterion.
- A 5G phone logs C-band for comparison.
- Verizon's public coverage map at the same address is the baseline.
Fix catalogue: LTE Network Extender, external antenna kit, 5G Home/Business Internet, Fios. Optional: IoT Network Continuity eSIM for alarm and IoT endpoints.
Model: gradient-boosted demarc-loss predictor. Features are building type and era (NJ MOD-IV class and year built, Rutgers building inventory), floor or below-grade location, and outdoor predicted signal. It is scored leave-one-building-out.
- **Verizon money:** Cost of services: failed installs and repeat dispatches avoided. Industry estimates put a truck roll at $150-600; Verizon's figure is undisclosed.
Equipment and accessory revenue: Network Extenders and antennas sold where they are truly needed, Schulman's own one-third-cost example.
Retention: homes whose box fails indoors are the ones that leave for cable VoIP.
Regulatory: indoor evidence behind 63.71(f)(2) certifications in a docket the FCC has already paused once.
CFO formula (labeled assumptions): value = lines migrated x share needing an indoor fix x (repeat-roll probability x roll cost + churn probability x ARPU x lifetime) - fix cost.
Illustration: 10,000 lines x 15% x (0.8 x $250 + 0.1 x $40 x 36) is about $0.5M per 10,000 lines. Honest: modest per wire center, but it scales across 1,763 wire centers.
- **Dated anchor:** - Oct 15 2026: new 63.71(f)(2)(ii) takes effect (FCC 26-19, 91 FR 62334). It certifies adequacy from 1.7004(c)(3) parameters on outdoor maps at 1.5 m.
- Sept 8 2026: NJ copper notice.
- Sept 25 2026: DA 26-1038 pause.
- Aug 14 2026: VoiceConnect named.
The rule text is six days old, and LLM-default teams still describe the pre-2026 adequate-replacement test.
- **Primary data:** (1) Paired curb-versus-demarc measurements at 40+ telecom entrance rooms, basements and back offices. Sites are Busch and Livingston buildings in Piscataway (with Facilities/OIT permission) plus consenting student and staff homes in Piscataway and New Brunswick. Each gets a 24-h LTE-M log, BPC100 VoLTE registration and 20 test calls, and a photo of what sits at the demarc (alarm panel, fax, medical base).
(2) The first public dataset of demarc loss against Verizon's own outdoor map class, by building type and era.
(3) The 'ten-second verdict' check: for multi-carrier radios that pick a carrier at power-up (NAPCO), compare a 10-s snapshot with 24-h reliability at the same spot.
All of it is measured inside a wire center Verizon has noticed for retirement.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (20-s beat):
- On screen, Verizon's public map at a Piscataway address: covered.
- On the table, a 'basement' built from a real cinder block, rebar mesh and a steel door panel.
- Drop the LTE-M probe and a BPC100 inside and close the lid. The live dashboard shows RSRP falling about 20 dB, SINR going negative, VoLTE registration lost and the test call failing.
- Caption: 'FCC test: PASS. Basement: FAIL.'
- Thread a 3 m antenna lead through a hole in the block and the dashboard turns green.
- Cut to the map of the retiring wire center, colored by predicted need for an indoor fix, with our measured points on top.
POSTER TABLE: a judge picks any address in the Piscataway wire center. The model predicts 'self-install OK / antenna / tech visit' and shows the measured result for the nearest building of that type. Judges can try the box themselves.
BOM under $700.
- **Why unique:** - It is built on rule text that took effect six days ago, and on a 1.5 m outdoor-map definition other teams will not read.
- It measures at the demarc, where copper devices actually live. That is not generic indoor coverage, and not glass loss (our explored Glass Ceiling idea).
- The output is a planning artifact the network judges use (an address list per wire center), not a consumer app.
- It supplies the 'signal here' line on the Plug-and-Prove receipt.
- **Biggest risk:** - Verizon has MDT and crowdsourced indoor data from millions of phones and may say it already knows indoor loss.
- 40 sites is a small sample, and getting into basements in 18 days depends on Facilities.
- Verizon LTE may penetrate fine. The story then becomes 'proof for certification', which is weaker drama.
- Wire-center boundaries for Rutgers buildings are unverified, so the poster must say 'in a retiring wire center' only where proven.
- Close in spirit to our explored indoor-coverage ideas.

## I49: Kiss-Off Check: the alarm panel says 'sent', Verizon's box hears the answer, or reports that it didn't (lens tactile_demo, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (an on-device signal classifier that turns silent failures into tickets automatically). Pillar 3 is secondary., self-score 70)

**One-liner:** On Verizon's copper replacement, alarms work only if someone keys the alarm company's number into the box (##21*number#). The BPC100 supports only Contact ID, and Verizon's guide says 'Failure to verify may result in your alarm not working'. Verizon's own mailed swap guide never mentions the alarm. Kiss-Off Check is a $40 inline listener now and gateway firmware later. It hears every alarm call, names the format, checks that the number is registered, and confirms the central station's kiss-off tone, then reports pass or fail to Verizon over ThingSpace. It rides on the panel's own periodic test report, so it adds no synthetic test traffic.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED:
(1) Voice Connect 2nd Gen ('BPC100 User Guide'):
- 'Alarm companies use different protocols... In order to maximize the likelihood that the alarm panel can successfully connect to the alarm company, it is necessary to enter several codes into the Voice Connect device... dial ##21*MDN#... To ensure you programmed the alarm number correctly you should test your alarm... Failure to verify may result in your alarm not working.'
- 'Verizon is not responsible for unsuccessful alarm responses.'
https://www.verizon.com/content/dam/verizon/support/consumer/documents/phone/voice-connect-user-guide-2nd-gen.pdf
(2) The swap guide Verizon mails for the T2050-to-BPC100 upgrade contains zero mentions of alarm, medical or ##21 (our text search). INFERENCE TO TEST: if the numbers are stored in the device, every swap silently drops alarm routing. https://www.verizon.com/content/dam/verizon/support/consumer/documents/phone/voice-connect-upgrade-instructions.pdf
(3) The BPC100 shop footnote says only the Contact-ID alarm protocol is supported and other central-station protocols are not. https://www.verizon.com/business/shop/products/devices/connected-devices/inseego-business-phone-connect/
(4) FCC 26-19 (rules effective Oct 15 2026) declines AICC's condition that replacements 'support G.711 codec handshake with RFC 2833 disabled on calls to... life safety alarm monitoring receivers'. It cites 'five million installed [alarm] panels that use dialer-based protocols' and says only 'we encourage carriers to ensure their IP networks are appropriately configured so as to prevent alarm signaling failures.' https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-26-19A1.pdf
(5) Verizon's wording: 'many legacy alarm systems' (https://www.verizon.com/copper/) and 'compatible with most monitored home security systems' (https://www.verizon.com/support/consumer/homephone/voice-connect).
(6) Precedent: Verizon's 2014 Voice Link filing says 'some (but not all) other monitoring systems like alarm systems' may not be compatible, and 17% of landline keepers keep it for home security (FCC 14-185). In 2021, about 40% of roughly 6M 3G alarm radios were on Verizon (AICC 2021 petition, https://tma.us/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AICC-Petition-for-Emergency-Relief-5-10-21-FINAL.pdf).
- **Verizon tech:** - BPC100/Voice Connect RJ31X alarm port and the VoLTE path, the device-side ##21 registration table, and Verizon MDM.
- Listener: an MCU with a high-impedance isolated line tap and audio ADC running Goertzel/FFT detectors. It recognizes the Contact ID handshake (1400 + 2300 Hz) and DTMF frames, SIA FSK, 4/2 pulse, and the 1400 Hz kiss-off, then classifies the format and outcome.
- It reports through a ThingSpace self-serve LTE-M radio ($1.50/mo) using SMS or Connectivity Management callbacks, independent of the voice path being judged.
- Lab central station: Asterisk AlarmReceiver on a real number.
- Production path: the same detector as Inseego/BPC100 firmware, with results in Verizon MDM, so the whole Voice Connect fleet reports alarm health.
- **Verizon money:** Mostly retention plus cost of services.
- Alarm-bearing voice lines are the stickiest landline customers (17% of keepers). A silent failure at migration or swap pushes them to the alarm dealer's own cellular radio.
- Fewer escalations and repeat tech visits.
- Compliance optics: it does exactly what the FCC 'encouraged'.
CFO formula (labeled assumptions): value = migrated or swapped lines x alarm share (17%) x measured failure rate x (repeat-visit cost + churn probability x ARPU x lifetime).
No dollar figure is claimed until the bench measures the failure rate. Honest size: small to moderate.
- **Dated anchor:** - Oct 15 2026: FCC 26-19 rules take effect after the FCC refused alarm-codec protections.
- Oct 23 2026: B1/key-system lines (DA 26-891).
- Verizon's live 2026 copper FAQ says 'many legacy alarm systems'.
- The non-obvious discovery is the ##21 registration step plus a swap guide that never mentions alarms. It is only findable by reading Verizon's own PDFs.
- **Primary data:** (1) Format-survival matrix on Verizon's production network. Panels: 8-12 secondhand units, among them Honeywell/Ademco Vista, DSC PowerSeries, Napco Gemini, Interlogix NX, and the Fire-Lite and Silent Knight 5104 DACTs, which Rutgers' March 2025 design standard still specifies. Formats: Contact ID, SIA, 4/2 and modem. Paths: a copper simulator, and the BPC100 with and without ##21 registration. 100 trials each, with kiss-off latency recorded.
(2) The swap test: register numbers on one BPC100, swap the SIM/line to a second unit per Verizon's guide, then trip the alarm.
(3) Classifier accuracy (a confusion matrix) on those recordings.
Nobody has published any of this.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (20-s beat):
- A judge presses PANIC on a 1990s keypad wired to Verizon's BPC100. About 6 s later their phone shows 'PANIC zone 3 — kiss-off received over Verizon VoLTE'.
- We follow Verizon's own swap guide on camera to a new box. A test call works.
- PANIC again. The keypad still shows 'COMM OK/sent', but the listener's LED goes red and the dashboard reads: 'Alarm call to unregistered number: no kiss-off. Fix sent: ##21*...#'.
- Caption: 'The phone works. The alarm doesn't. Now Verizon knows.'
POSTER TABLE: a live trial counter and the matrix. Judges choose a panel and a format and press the button.
BOM under $1,200, mostly used panels.
GATE: the swap beat runs only if the bench confirms that registrations do not survive a swap. If they survive, the video uses the format beat (Contact ID versus SIA).
- **Why unique:** - It is built on Verizon's own instructions, footnote and swap guide, plus an FCC refusal that took effect six days ago.
- It is passive: it uses the panel's own test timer, with no synthetic calls and no audio stored.
- It targets the FCC's 5 million residential and SMB dialer panels, not campus life-safety lines.
- Kiss-off tones and ##21 registration are deep domain detail an LLM-default team will not reach.
- **Biggest risk:** - Adjacent to Last Copper's roadmap alarm harness and to peer device-check ideas.
- Many alarms already moved to cellular communicators after the 3G sunset, which shrinks the market.
- The alarm dealer, not Verizon, owns most fixes.
- If registrations survive swaps and most formats pass, the drama is gone and the value becomes proof rather than rescue.
- We must never dial a real central station without its permission.
- An inline tap on a customer line in production needs Verizon or Inseego to absorb it into firmware.

## I50: Window Seat: 60 cm of receiver placement, bought with Verizon Dollars, returns FWA sector capacity (lens tactile_demo, pillar Pillar 2: Cost & Sustainability Transformation (capacity without capex or spectrum), with Pillar 3 (faster home internet) as secondary., self-score 63)

**One-liner:** Moved 60 cm from behind the TV to the window, the same 5G Home receiver can need a fraction of the cell's airtime per gigabyte, because link adaptation turns SINR into bits per hertz. Window Seat helps Verizon find the worst-placed receivers on its busiest fixed-wireless sectors. Verizon then pays those customers in Verizon Dollars (launched Jun 16 2026) to move the receiver with a 2-minute live-signal guide. The freed airtime lets Verizon sell more FWA homes on the same sector without new spectrum or sites.

- **Problem evidence:** VERIFIED:
(1) Verizon Q2 2026 (Jul 24 2026): 193,000 FWA net adds, and 'approximately 17.1 million fixed wireless access and fiber broadband connections'. https://www.verizon.com/about/news/verizon-delivers-record-2q26-results
(2) Verizon Dollars (3% back monthly) launched Jun 16 2026 with Simplicity and Loyalty: https://www.verizon.com/about/news/verizon-puts-customers-first
(3) Schulman (Q1'26) favors fixing the place over replacing the phone; a femtocell at 'one-third the cost': https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-06/vz_1q26_transcript_042726rm.pdf
(4) Vestberg (May 2024): 'I'm probably using on average 80 megahertz on the 160'. Capacity is NOT binding everywhere, which is our honest caveat. https://broadbandbreakfast.com/verizon-ceo-says-company-has-room-to-grow-fixed-wireless.md
PER SEARCH SUMMARY ONLY, NOT FETCHED:
- Verizon targets 8-9M FWA subscribers by 2028 (5.1M in mid-2025): https://convergedigest.com/verizon-eyes-8-9m-fwa-subs-by-2028-frontier-close-in-2026/
- Verizon manages FWA capacity sector by sector (Adam Koeppe): https://www.fierce-network.com/tech/verizons-adam-koeppe-talks-about-capacity-fixed-wireless
- CFO Skiadas (Mar 2026) said Verizon entered 2026 with more FWA capacity and more 'open-for-sale' homes. The verbatim quote is UNVERIFIED.
Physics: 3GPP TS 38.214 CQI tables span about 0.15-5.5 bit/s/Hz per layer, so a receiver at low SINR uses several times more resource blocks per GB than one at high SINR.
- **Verizon tech:** - Verizon 5G Home/LTE Home receivers on C-band n77 (mmWave where present), and the per-receiver RSRP/SINR/band telemetry visible in the receiver UI and the Verizon Home app.
- RAN scheduler PRB accounting and sector-load counters (Verizon-internal, for targeting).
- Verizon Dollars as the incentive currency.
- Prototype on Verizon's production 5G network: the receiver on a linear rail logging SINR, MCS, rank and throughput, with a live 'airtime per GB' estimate derived from the measured spectral efficiency.
- **Verizon money:** Consumer and Business FWA revenue (broadband lines), plus capex and spectrum avoided.
CFO formula (labeled assumptions): value = capacity-limited sectors x freed airtime share x homes sellable per unit airtime x ARPU x 12.
- Illustration only: 2,000 constrained sectors x 5% freed x 200 homes per sector x $50 x 12 is about $120M/yr of sellable capacity. Every input except ARPU is an assumption, and Vestberg's 80-of-160-MHz remark suggests the constrained share may be small.
- Incentive cost: a one-time $5-10 in Verizon Dollars per moved receiver.
Honest: potentially large but unproven, because it depends on Verizon-internal sector data we cannot see.
- **Dated anchor:** - Jun 16 2026: Verizon Dollars launched.
- Jul 24 2026: Q2'26 results with 193K FWA adds and 17.1M FWA plus fiber.
- Mar 2026: Skiadas comments on FWA capacity (UNVERIFIED verbatim).
This is the weakest post-cutoff anchor of the five. Its novelty is using a June 2026 loyalty currency to buy spectral efficiency.
- **Primary data:** (1) 10-15 consenting off-campus households with Verizon 5G Home in New Brunswick and Piscataway, plus 1-2 team-owned receivers. At the current spot and 5-8 candidate spots, morning and evening, record SINR, RSRP, MCS, rank and throughput.
(2) A photo census of where students actually put the receiver (behind the TV, in a closet, in the basement).
(3) A time-to-best-spot usability test of a 2-minute guide.
(4) Building-material notes for New Brunswick's old wood-frame and brick housing stock.
Together these give the first measured 'airtime multiplier' distribution for real student-housing placements.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (20-s beat):
- A 5G Home receiver sits on a 1 m rail behind a mock wall of drywall and an old window.
- The dashboard reads SINR 4 dB, airtime per GB 3.2x.
- A judge slides it 60 cm to the window: SINR 16 dB, airtime per GB 1.0x.
- Caption: 'That slide just made room on the cell for about 2 more homes at 9 pm.' A Verizon Dollars badge pops: '+$5 for moving your receiver.'
POSTER TABLE: judges slide it themselves, with a map of measured houses and their before and after.
BOM under $500 plus 1-2 months of service.
- **Why unique:** - It frames receiver placement as sector-capacity economics for network planners, not as consumer UX.
- It pairs a June 2026 loyalty currency with link-adaptation physics.
- It uses measurements from real student housing.
- It is the one idea in this set outside the copper transition, which adds portfolio diversity.
- **Biggest risk:** - Verizon already guides placement at setup in the Home app and may already know the SINR distribution of every receiver.
- Capacity may not bind on most sectors (Vestberg: about 80 of 160 MHz used in 2024). Scheduler-fairness math may be contested.
- Recruiting households in 18 days.
- Overlaps our explored rental and FWA ideas (Turnover, Lease-Ready, Glass Ceiling).
- The weakest post-cutoff anchor of the five.

## I51: Substantially Similar: per-address proof that Verizon's copper replacement works (lens verizon_programs, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency (automates the evidence behind Verizon's own discontinuance certification). Pillar 2 is secondary: it unlocks copper O&M savings sooner., self-score 70)

**One-liner:** Since Oct 15, 2026, Verizon can have a legacy-voice discontinuance auto-granted if it certifies that every location has a qualifying replacement. The FCC has already pulled Verizon's 9-state application (NJ included) from auto-grant after a wave of objections. Substantially Similar gives Verizon per-address evidence: indoor 5/1 Mbps, VoLTE call success and voice quality, and runtime when the power fails. It predicts that evidence for every location in a retiring wire center and turns each failing address into the cheapest fix before anyone files an objection. Every VoiceConnect install then adds a new labeled test point.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) The FCC 26-19 amendments to 47 CFR 63.71 and 63.602 took effect Oct 15, 2026 (91 FR 62334, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf). Under new 63.71(f)(2), auto-grant follows if the carrier 'certifies that in every location throughout the affected service area' at least one of these exists: facilities-based VoIP; 'facilities-based mobile wireless service operating at speeds of at least 5 Mbps download and 1 Mbps upload, consistent with the coverage parameters set forth in § 1.7004(c)(3)'; or a voice service the carrier 'certifies offers substantially similar levels of network performance and availability ... based on the applicant's own internal network testing'. The old 63.602 adequate-replacement test is removed. BDC mobile maps are outdoor or in-vehicle only (47 CFR 1.7004(c)(5), per research digest). (2) Verizon's WC 26-227 (legacy TDM voice in portions of 9 states, including NJ; filed Aug 10, 2026; DA 26-890, https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-890A1.pdf) was pulled from auto-grant on Sept 25, 2026 by DA 26-1038: 'will not be automatically granted. The Commission requires additional time' (https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1038A1.pdf). (3) Our ECFS API pull counts 615 filings in 26-227 by Oct 20, 2026 (https://www.fcc.gov/ecfs/search/search-filings/results?q=(proceedings.name:(%2226-227%22))). They include the NJ BPU Office of Cable TV & Telecommunications (Sept 10), Township of Hanover NJ, Borough of Franklin Lakes NJ, a Verizon reply (Sept 24), a Verizon supplemental letter (Oct 9) and a Rappahannock County response (Oct 16). Filing titles include 'There is NO cellphone coverage on our property' and '...if there is no power there is no way to call out'. (4) Secondary sources: the first filing covered about 277,000 lines, and a Sept 9 filing (WC 26-252) covers about 750,000 more locations. Verizon says 'no one will lose service until a reliable alternative' is available (Alarm Grid, Oct 13, 2026, https://www.alarmgrid.com/blog/2026/10). Customers are told to call the number on their letter to learn what works at their address (WDBJ, Sept 15, 2026, https://www.wdbj7.com/2026/09/15/end-traditional-landlines-what-shift-away-copper-means-you/). (5) Verizon's own life-safety partner MarketSpark documents sites that showed 'full bars' yet failed because of poor RSRQ (https://www.marketspark.com/understanding-cellular-signal-quality/). (6) Copper is in the 2026 cost program: CFO Skiadas, Jan 30, 2026 ('$5 billion of cost transformation ... decommission of legacy elements in the network, including copper', https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-02/vz_4q25_transcript_013026r.pdf). The only dollar figure Verizon has published is from 2022: about $180M a year from 36 all-fiber COs, 'in part driven by a reduction in technician dispatches' (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2022-03/Investor-Day-2022-Transcript.pdf). (7) Scale: 2026 notices name 1,763 unique wire-center CLLIs across 21 states, 1,432 of them Frontier (https://www.verizon.com/about/terms-conditions/network-disclosures). NJ notice 2026-03-A-NJ lists 62 wire centers 'on or after December 10, 2026' (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/verizon-new-jersey-copper-retirement-notice-2026-0908.pdf).
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon's copper-replacement voice paths: VoiceConnect / Wireless Home Phone (VoLTE), Business Phone Connect BPC100, Fios Digital Voice and One Talk. Also Verizon LTE / 5G UW coverage and its BDC mobile coverage filings, 5G Home and LTE Home FWA as the fallback for failing addresses, and Verizon's own floor-level indoor performance analytics (CTO, Jun 24, 2026, https://www.verizon.com/about/news/verizon-architecting-network-autonomy), which the model would consume inside Verizon. Packaged as a per-address 'replacement readiness' service that could be exposed CAMARA-style (Connectivity Insights) through Aduna.
- **Verizon money:** Two 10-K lines. Cost of services ($27.8B in FY2025) through copper O&M and dispatch savings, and legacy wireline. Labeled assumption: if the 2022 figure ($180M/yr / 36 COs) scales, a fully retired CO saves about $5M/yr, so each month that objections delay the 62 NJ wire centers costs on the order of $26M. That is an assumption, not a Verizon figure. We do not claim the savings, only faster and cleaner certification, plus fewer failed migrations and repeat truck rolls. FWA or Fios capture at addresses where mobile voice fails adds to Consumer and Business broadband revenue. The 2.7% subscription figure in the WC 26-252 vendor report means the at-risk base is about 1M locations. Honest size: material, but depends on how much the FCC weighs objection evidence.
- **Dated anchor:** Several dates sit inside the next five weeks. FCC 26-19's 63.71(f)(2) certification rule has been in force since Oct 15, 2026 (FR Oct 1, 2026). DA 26-1038 (Sept 25, 2026) pulled the NJ-inclusive application from auto-grant. DA 26-1061 (Oct 1, 2026) opened master objection docket WC 26-214. Verizon's Oct 9 supplemental letter and Rappahannock's Oct 16 response are in the docket. The NJ copper notice date is Dec 10, 2026. Verizon's Q3 call is Oct 26, 2026, also before the Nov 8 video.
- **Primary data:** (a) Rutgers sits inside at least one and probably three of Verizon's 62 retiring NJ wire centers. Busch, Livingston, RBHS and SHI are in Piscataway (PSWYNJPI); the Rutgers Marine Field Station is in Little Egg Harbor (LEHTNJ01); the Haskin Shellfish Research Lab is in Port Norris (MCTWNJPN). The serving wire center for each remote site is UNVERIFIED and must be confirmed from OIT bills or CSRs. That gives a stratified test set from campus buildings to a marsh-road field station: indoor 5/1 Mbps, VoLTE call setup and drops, MOS from recorded test calls, and runtime during a power cut on a VoiceConnect-class device. (b) A consented panel of commuter-student households in 7 nearby retiring wire centers (Piscataway, Metuchen, Dunellen, South River, Fords, Carteret, Linden, all on the NJ notice). Each household runs a phone-based test at the landline jack and reports what is plugged into the line (alarm, fax, medical alert, TTY). (c) A load test during an SHI Stadium home game, since SHI is in the Piscataway wire center. (d) An LLM classification of all 615 public 26-227 filings by objection type and county, matched to the evidence that answers each type. No CPNI is used.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): The new rule text appears on screen, then Verizon's 62-wire-center NJ map with three Rutgers pins. A student in the Marine Field Station kitchen shows the BDC map 'covered' while the indoor test fails: a VoLTE call drops and throughput is under 5/1. The model then paints every address in the Piscataway wire center green, amber or red. Amber addresses get a fix list (window antenna, 5G Home, Fios, or keep copper). We cut the power and the runtime timer starts. The closing beat: '615 objections, sorted; here is the evidence for each.' POSTER TABLE: a VoiceConnect-class base or BPC100 runs a live test call with an on-screen MOS score and a power-cut timer, next to an interactive wire-center map that judges can click by address. The national roll-up covers 1,763 wire centers.
- **Why unique:** The rule that makes per-location certification Verizon's job took effect six days ago, so no LLM-default team will know it. Our evidence comes from places only Rutgers can get into: field stations inside retiring wire centers, plus commuter households across the retiring NJ belt. The pitch is about Verizon's biggest live transformation program and its newest regulatory exposure, not a campus app. It does not depend on Rutgers having any copper lines left.
- **Biggest risk:** (1) Verizon may not want evidence beyond the legal standard (BDC 5/1), because adverse results could be used against it. (2) Verizon's internal drive-test and floor-level data are far richer than ours, so a judge may say 'we already do this.' (3) Our sample is small. (4) VoiceConnect may only be sold to discontinuance customers, so we may have to use a BPC100 or phone as a proxy. (5) Which wire center serves each Rutgers remote site is unverified. (6) It overlaps our own Gen 3 'Glass Ceiling' indoor-measurement idea.

## I52: ECRC Zero: every PBX 911 call reaches the right PSAP when New Jersey goes NG911 (lens verizon_programs, pillar Pillar 1: AI-Driven Automation & Efficiency, by automating Verizon's outreach and data-intake process that the FCC requires (an agent plus a validator). Pillar 3 is secondary: verified 911 location becomes a selling point of Verizon IP voice., self-score 69)

**One-liner:** In Verizon's NG911 rollout, 911 calls from legacy-PBX customers whose location sits with a third-party PS/ALI provider go to a national relay center, where the caller must say where they are. The FCC gave Verizon 45 days (to about Nov 12, 2026) to reach every such customer. ECRC Zero does four things: it finds the person who actually owns 911 location inside a large, merged enterprise; it converts and GIS-validates that enterprise's records into Verizon's PS/ALI template; it proves the routing in a lab NG911 core; and it gives Verizon a daily triage of every call that still lands at the relay center. Rutgers (moving from Cisco to Teams, with legacy UMDNJ numbers on Verizon's Piscataway switch and its own PSAP) becomes the first enterprise proven end to end before NJ's Lumen-built ESInet goes live.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) DA 26-1042 (Sept 28, 2026, https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1042A1.pdf). In First Office Application testing in Massachusetts on Jan 12, 2026, Verizon found that 911 calls from TDM 'dialtone customers' using third-party PS/ALI 'were delivered to Intrado's Emergency Call Relay Center (ECRC) rather than directly to' the NGCS, because 'Verizon does not have the MLTS customer's location information'. The caller must 'verbally communicate with a telecommunicator at the national call center'. The waiver conditions require Verizon, 'within 45 days', to 'use its best efforts to individually contact all of its MLTS customers who use PS/ALI services' and to report 'the number of calls ... routed to Intrado's ECRC' at 60 and 120 days. Massachusetts told the FCC that Verizon's notice 'has been ineffective' (fn 84). Footnote 44 lists why some customers are unreachable: contact data is 'outdated', 'merger activity or corporate reorganization has moved the customer's responsibility for 911 location management', the call came from 'a device in a public space', or 'call forwarding from a security desk'. Verizon says the issue 'extends to all jurisdictions in which Verizon has TDM-based MLTS customers that use a PS/ALI service'. (2) DA 26-1043 (Sept 28, 2026, https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-1043A1.pdf) puts all wireline OSPs on notice, 'encourage[s] TDM-based MLTS users to consider transitioning to IP-based systems' and encourages 'OSPs to provide incentives' to do so. (3) Verizon's fix is a manual one: customers 'copy your existing PS/ALI data' into an Excel template and email it to psaliupdate@verizon.com, with validation 'typically ... within one business day' (https://www.verizon.com/business/support/product-support/products/wireline-911-location-management/). (4) NJ's statewide ESInet is being built by Lumen under a 2022 seven-year contract covering 248 PSAPs in 21 counties (https://s21.q4cdn.com/756714007/files/doc_news/Lumen-wins-Next-Gen-911-contract-in-New-Jersey-2022.pdf). NJ is not in the waiver appendix, so its Phase 1 is still ahead; the timing is UNVERIFIED. (5) The PBX Trunk Service and ISDN BRI discontinuance (WC 26-228, NJ included) is authorized 'on or after October 23, 2026' (DA 26-891, https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/DA-26-891A1.pdf). Its grant status is UNVERIFIED.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon's wireline NG911 delivery through Intrado (Phase 1 SIP, Phase 2 PIDF-LO location), its PS/ALI update template and validation pipeline, the Intrado ECRC daily report Verizon now reviews (Sept 8 ex parte), and the IP voice destinations that end TDM PS/ALI for good: Verizon SIP trunking, One Talk and Verizon's Microsoft Teams Operator Connect offer (offered since 2022; current status to confirm). The lab demo emulates the NJ side: a Kamailio-based mini-ESInet with a LoST/ECRF mock and PIDF-LO.
- **Verizon money:** Enterprise & Public Sector revenue was $13.5B in FY2025, down 4.8%. Every TDM MLTS customer squeezed at once by WC 26-228 trunk discontinuance and by NG911 PS/ALI is a forced-migration moment: retain and upgrade the customer to Verizon IP voice with verified 911, or lose them to a cloud-UC rival. The compliance side covers waiver conditions, ECRC-call reporting to the FCC, and lower enforcement and headline risk on 911 misroutes. Honest size: Verizon's count of PS/ALI customers is not public, so we cannot size the revenue. Per enterprise, voice contracts run from tens of thousands to millions of dollars a year. Labeled assumptions only.
- **Dated anchor:** DA 26-1042 and DA 26-1043 were released Sept 28, 2026. Verizon's 45-day individual-contact deadline falls about Nov 12, 2026, the 60-day ECRC report about Nov 27, 2026, and the 120-day report about Jan 26, 2027, which is days before the Feb 3 poster. The WC 26-228 PBX-trunk discontinuance date is on or after Oct 23, 2026. Rutgers' Teams Phone launched Feb 4, 2026 (https://it.rutgers.edu/voice/microsoft-teams-phone/). FCC 26-63 NOI comments on modernizing the 911 framework are due Nov 16, 2026.
- **Primary data:** (a) Rutgers' 911 MLTS map. Number ranges by carrier: the legacy 732-235/445/932/743 blocks sit on Verizon NJ's Piscataway 5ESS PSWYNJPIDS5 (https://localcallingguide.com/xmlprefix.php?npa=732&nxx=235), and the main 848-445/932 blocks sit on PAETEC/Windstream (https://www.localcallingguide.com/lca_prefix.php?npa=848&nxx=445). The OIT export would also give ERLs, the Teams LIS configuration and whether any third-party PS/ALI is used, with OIT cooperation. (b) A footnote-44 audit of a real merged enterprise: legacy UMDNJ/RBHS units after the 2013 merger, public-space and courtesy phones, security-desk forwarding, and Cisco desk phones left '911-only' after users move to Teams (verified on the OIT page). (c) GIS validation of every Rutgers building address against NJ address points and municipal and PSAP boundaries, measuring the share that would misroute. Example: Busch and Livingston buildings carrying a 'New Brunswick' postal city while sitting in Piscataway. (d) Coordinated lab test calls, and later RUSSECC test calls, with Rutgers' own PSAP. Never a live 911 call without PSAP coordination.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): A desk phone on Busch 'dials 911' into our lab NG911 core. The screen shows the call landing at a mock 'National Relay Center: Caller, what is your address?' and a 20-second clock running. The agent ingests Rutgers' messy ERL and building list, finds the owner (an email chain resolves 'RBHS legacy number range belongs to X'), fixes 'New Brunswick' to 'Piscataway', adds floor and room, and emits Verizon's template. We redial and the call routes to the correct PSAP with a PIDF-LO 'Hill Center, Floor 3'. Overlay text: the FCC's 45-day order and 'ECRC calls: 37 to 0'. POSTER TABLE: two IP phones, the mini-ESInet on a laptop, and a Verizon-side dashboard that clusters a mock daily ECRC report by number range, then enterprise, then accountable owner, then outreach status.
- **Why unique:** It is built on an FCC order released 23 days ago that names Verizon and gives it hard deadlines inside our competition window. No LLM-default team will find it. Rutgers matches every risk factor in footnote 44: a merged enterprise, a PBX migration, public-space phones and its own PSAP. That makes it the perfect proving ground. It is enterprise wireline plumbing, not wireless caller location, so it is distinct from Floor Truth and RightRoom.
- **Biggest risk:** (1) Rutgers may not be a Verizon PS/ALI customer at all, since its main numbers sit with a CLEC. The pitch then becomes 'proven on Rutgers' data model' rather than 'Rutgers fixed'. (2) The demo needs OIT data, and RUSSECC tests need consent. (3) NJ's Phase 1 timing is unknown. (4) Verizon may already be building an internal intake tool. (5) Judges may lump it with the crowded '911 location' theme.

## I53: Skiles Avenue Edge: measuring whether Verizon's copper-emptied central offices can actually serve AI 2 km away (lens verizon_programs, pillar Pillar 2: Cost & Sustainability Transformation, turning stranded copper real estate into revenue. Pillar 3 is secondary: low-latency inference as a product., self-score 64)

**One-liner:** Verizon is turning central offices it is emptying of copper into AI inference edge sites, and its first trial 'sold out ... in 24 hours'. The Piscataway CO at 4 Skiles Ave, 2 km from Busch, is in the Dec 10 wave. Skiles Avenue measures three things. First, where Verizon 5G and Fios traffic from Busch actually leaves Verizon's network today, and how many milliseconds local breakout plus Quality-on-Demand would save. Second, real inference demand at Rutgers labs, RBHS, HELIX and the new NJ Supercomputer. Third, a ranking of all 1,763 wire centers retiring in 2026 by how much a converted CO would truly deliver.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) CEO Schulman on the Q2 2026 call (Jul 24, 2026, https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2026-08/vz_2q2026_transcript_072426rm.pdf): 'we have thousands of central offices, many of which we're taking copper out of, and we are retrofitting them to be remote data centers that are power-ready, permitted, fully redundant infrastructure ... we did a small trial on that and sold out capability in 24 hours'. He named the workloads: 'ultra-low latency, like robotics or remote surgery, autonomous driving'. He expects AI Connect to 'noticeably contribute to our revenue growth starting next year', with margins 'equal to or greater than our existing margin structures'. (2) No CO-retrofit partners have been named (Fierce, https://www.fierce-network.com/broadband/verizon-touts-1b-deal-google-connect-ai-data-centers; RCR Jul 27, 2026, https://rcrwireless.com/20260727/carriers/verizon-google-dci-deal-2027-ai-cloud-edge). Ziply is doing similar CO retrofits (https://www.lightwaveonline.com/COretrrofit). (3) The NJ notice 2026-03-A-NJ lists Piscataway, 4 Skiles Ave (PSWYNJPI), 'on or after December 10, 2026' (https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/verizon-new-jersey-copper-retirement-notice-2026-0908.pdf). (4) Local siting is hostile: the New Brunswick City Council cancelled a proposed data center after protest on Feb 18, 2026 (https://www.dailytargum.com/article/new-brunswick-city-council-cancels-data-center-plans-in-response-to-protest-20260219). That makes already-permitted COs scarce assets. (5) Demand that Verizon is currently missing: the Rutgers and RWJBarnabas AI Innovation Hub at HELIX went to AWS (Sept 16, 2026, https://www.dailytargum.com/article/pou9nzfpro16-20260916). The NJ Supercomputer Initiative (>$125M, announced Oct 20, 2026) will be hosted in SHI International's NJ facilities, and Verizon is not in the announcement (https://www.rutgers.edu/news/rutgers-partnering-new-jersey-supercomputer-initiative-drive-innovation-solve-shared-statewide). (6) Rutgers' own data center lost power on Apr 27, 2026, taking down Rutgers.edu, myRutgers, payroll and student systems from 10:08 AM to 8:06 PM (https://it.rutgers.edu/alerts/2026/04/27/issues-with-network-connectivity-and-it-services/).
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon AI Connect (CO retrofits, lit and dark fiber), 5G SA with a distributed UPF for local breakout, CAMARA Quality-on-Demand through Aduna (Verizon is an equity partner) for latency guarantees to the CO edge, and Fios OLTs that already terminate in the CO. Measured over Verizon 5G UW and Fios with traceroute/mtr, RTT and jitter to campus, cloud regions and any live Verizon 5G Edge or Wavelength zone (status unverified). Roadmap: AI-RAN, sharing CO GPUs with vRAN pools (NVIDIA joined Verizon's 6G Innovation Forum on Sept 14, 2026).
- **Verizon money:** New AI Connect revenue (new revenue line from 2027), plus capex efficiency on 'success-based' CO conversions. Labeled assumption: 0.5-2 MW of usable power per converted CO at $150-250 per kW-month is about $0.9-6M a year per site. Verizon has not disclosed per-site economics. Our tool does not create the revenue. It prevents stranded edge capacity (a CO edge that cannot beat the cloud because traffic hairpins to a distant gateway) and orders conversions by measured benefit and anchor demand. Rutgers itself is a small anchor tenant: research inference, and DR after the Apr 27 outage.
- **Dated anchor:** Several dates are post-cutoff. The first public AI Connect and CO-retrofit statements came Jul 24, 2026. The Corning fiber deal for 'AI Connect long-haul backbone' was announced Sept 8, 2026 (https://www.verizon.com/about/news/verizon-corning-broadband-ai-fiber-agreement), and NVIDIA joined Verizon's 6GIF on Sept 14, 2026. The NJ Supercomputer was announced Oct 20, 2026 and the HELIX AI hub went to AWS on Sept 16, 2026. The Piscataway copper date is Dec 10, 2026, and Verizon's Q3 call (Oct 26) may add AI Connect detail.
- **Primary data:** (a) A latency and path atlas. From about 20 Busch, Livingston and RBHS points over Verizon 5G UW (and a Verizon Fios line in Piscataway), we measure where traffic exits Verizon's network today (first public hop, reverse DNS, latency triangulation). We also measure RTT and jitter to a campus server, to cloud regions and to the closest edge zone. The headline: how far a Busch-to-Busch packet travels over Verizon. (b) An inference-demand census of Rutgers labs (CAIT on Busch, robotics, WINLAB, RBHS imaging, HELIX/RWJ, Athletics video): workload, latency budget, data residency (HIPAA) and GPU-hours. (c) A robot-arm task-success experiment comparing cloud inference with near-campus inference. (d) Rutgers OIT's 2026 outage log (Apr 27, Aug 7, Oct 12) as the DR demand case.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): A drone shot runs from 4 Skiles Ave to Busch (2 km). A traceroute animation shows a Busch phone's packet travelling to a distant gateway and back (measured number). A robot arm on Busch grasps objects using inference first from the cloud (fails or slows), then from a 'CO edge' GPU on a local-breakout path (succeeds). Schulman's 'sold out in 24 hours' quote appears, then a national map of 1,763 retiring wire centers ranked by measured latency benefit times anchor demand, with Piscataway in the top tier. POSTER TABLE: a live Verizon 5G phone running the path test at the table, the robot arm with a latency slider (QoD on/off), and the clickable CO ranking map.
- **Why unique:** Verizon first described AI Connect and CO retrofits publicly in July 2026, after most LLM cutoffs. Nobody else will measure the 5G SA local-breakout prerequisite or tie a specific retiring CO to a specific university's demand. The project is about a Verizon growth leg, not a campus app, and it uses Rutgers as both measurement site and anchor tenant.
- **Biggest risk:** (1) Verizon planners already know where their UPFs and gateways are, so our path measurements may be 'old news' internally. (2) Edge-inference demand below 10 ms is debatable for most workloads, and AWS already won the HELIX hub. (3) Students cannot see CO power or space, so the per-CO ranking is outside-in. (4) The demo shows a simulated CO edge. (5) Data-center politics in NJ could extend to CO conversions.

## I54: Plug In, Prove It: the copper replacement that proves itself at install, with no truck roll (lens verizon_programs, pillar Pillar 2: Cost & Sustainability Transformation (fewer truck rolls, repeat tickets and failed migrations). Pillar 3 is secondary: the customer experience at a forced migration., self-score 61)

**One-liner:** Verizon says no one loses copper service until a reliable alternative works, and the FCC stopped requiring any compatibility test on Oct 15, 2026. Plug In, Prove It is a phone-guided self-install. It places the VoiceConnect base where the live signal is best, recognizes what is plugged into the old line (alarm panel, fax, medical-alert base, TTY), runs a device-specific test for each plus a 911 address readback, and closes the migration without a technician. It is backed by a Rutgers lab matrix that tells Verizon which device and path combinations fail and which network setting fixes them.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) FCC 26-19 removed 63.602, the adequate-replacement test that required 'interoperability and compatibility with an enumerated list of applications and functionalities', effective Oct 15, 2026 (91 FR 62334, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-10-01/pdf/2026-20192.pdf). It refused AICC's alarm-signal condition, noted that the 2016 device-compatibility requirement 'sunset in 2025', and only 'encourage[s] carriers to ensure their IP networks are appropriately configured so as to prevent alarm signaling failures', citing AICC's 'five million installed [alarm] panels' (https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-26-19A1.pdf). (2) Verizon's own precedent is FCC 14-185 (https://docs.fcc.gov/public/attachments/FCC-14-185A1.pdf). Voice Link 'may not be compatible' with devices built for copper. A survey cited there found that 24% keep a landline for a medical alert device and 17% for home security. (3) Verizon's BPC100 supports Contact-ID only, and 'other central-station protocols are not supported' (https://www.verizon.com/business/shop/products/devices/connected-devices/inseego-business-phone-connect/). (4) AICC: each migration is a truck roll of up to 3 hours, and about 25% of appointments get no access (https://tma.us/wp-content/uploads/2021/05/AICC-Petition-for-Emergency-Relief-5-10-21-FINAL.pdf). Verizon's copper savings are 'in part driven by a reduction in technician dispatches' (2022 Investor Day, https://www.verizon.com/about/sites/default/files/2022-03/Investor-Day-2022-Transcript.pdf). (5) Verizon says 'no one will lose service until a reliable alternative' exists, and that VoiceConnect offers battery backup and 911. Both statements are secondary: https://www.alarmgrid.com/blog/2026/10 (Oct 13, 2026) and WDBJ (https://www.wdbj7.com/2026/09/15/end-traditional-landlines-what-shift-away-copper-means-you/). (6) The two filings cover about 1M lines across 18 states (26-227 plus 26-252, secondary), and 615 objections have been filed in 26-227 alone.
- **Verizon tech:** Verizon VoiceConnect / Wireless Home Phone (VoLTE), BPC100 (Contact-ID over VoLTE), Fios Digital Voice and One Talk as the replacement paths. The phone reads RSRP/RSRQ/SINR to guide placement. The 911 check is a 933 address readback where Verizon supports it (UNVERIFIED), otherwise a registered-address confirmation. The lab finds per-path codec and DTMF-transport settings (G.711 passthrough, RFC 2833 handling) that Verizon could push as device or network profiles.
- **Verizon money:** Cost of services (FY2025 $27.8B) through dispatches, repeat trouble tickets and care calls. Labeled assumptions: $150-300 per avoided dispatch (industry range, not a Verizon figure), times about 1M lines in the 26-227 and 26-252 filings (secondary), times a 10-20 point gain in self-install success, comes to roughly $15-60M one-time. Secondary benefits: fewer objections (faster approvals and copper savings) and retention of migrating voice households.
- **Dated anchor:** The 63.602 compatibility test was removed effective Oct 15, 2026. DA 26-1038 paused auto-grant on Sept 25, 2026. Verizon's no-cutoff-without-alternative statements came in Sept and Oct 2026. The WC 26-228 business-line discontinuance is authorized on or after Oct 23, 2026, and NJ copper on or after Dec 10, 2026.
- **Primary data:** (a) Older-adult volunteers from Rutgers' Osher Lifelong Learning Institute (recruitment to confirm), the population that dominates the 26-227 objections. We collect a census of what is plugged into their phone lines, then run timed self-install trials with and without the app, measuring success rate, time and errors. (b) The commuter-student household panel in 7 retiring NJ wire centers, same protocol. (c) A Rutgers ECE lab matrix testing legacy devices against Verizon replacement paths: Contact-ID and SIA alarm panels into a software receiver, fax (T.30), TTY, a PERS base and a dial-up POS terminal. Each pair gets 100+ automated transactions, and an AI classifier reads handshake audio to name the failure cause (codec, jitter, DTMF drop, echo canceller).
