- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): An OLLI volunteer unboxes the base. The phone says 'Move it to the kitchen window: +9 dB.' The camera recognizes a Honeywell alarm panel and a medical-alert base. A test signal arrives at our receiver dashboard, followed by a fax test page and the address readback. A green 'Proven at this address' certificate appears with the caption 'no truck'. Cut to the lab matrix: 'SIA over path X fails 31%; fixed by profile Y.' POSTER TABLE: a live rig with alarm panel, fax and medical-alert base on a Verizon replacement device, a receiver screen and a judge-triggered test with a pass/fail matrix.
- **Why unique:** The anchor is the FCC removing the compatibility test six days ago, which no default LLM knows. It targets Verizon's consumer and SMB install economics, not building lifelines, and it uses a Rutgers population (OLLI older adults) and a Rutgers lab that other teams will not think to recruit.
- **Biggest risk:** (1) It overlaps the champion's PROVE idea (end-to-end proof that a device works over the new path). (2) Verizon's apps already have self-install guides, so the delta must be the testing. (3) Real alarm tests need a monitoring-center or receiver partner. (4) Verizon's support for 933 is unverified. (5) Usability results from 20-30 volunteers by Nov 8 will be thin. (6) The truck-roll cost is an assumption.

## I55: Re-Select: win back the IoT devices other networks are abandoning, with Verizon's new IoT Network Continuity eSIM (lens verizon_programs, pillar Pillar 3: Next-Gen Products & Services (enterprise IoT continuity). Pillar 1 is secondary: an AI network-selection policy., self-score 55)

**One-liner:** T-Mobile switched off 2G on Aug 3, 2026 and has required special approval for new LTE-only business activations since Jan 1, 2026. Multi-carrier IoT devices pick a network once, at power-up. Re-Select audits Rutgers' cellular IoT fleet and logs all three networks at every device location for two weeks. It then uses Verizon's new IoT Network Continuity eSIM and ThingSpace APIs to move stranded and at-risk devices to Verizon, re-checks every night, and turns every location where Verizon loses into a network-quality ticket.

- **Problem evidence:** (1) T-Mobile shut down 2G GSM on Aug 3, 2026, and 'Beginning January 1, 2026, T-Mobile already began requiring special approval for new LTE-only or 5G Non-Standalone device activations for business customers'. LTE refarming by about 2028 is a reported leak and is secondary (Tech Times, Jul 7, 2026, https://www.techtimes.com/articles/319869/20260707/t-mobile-2g-gsm-ends-august-3-iot-operators-face-two-migration-waves-not-one.htm). (2) Verizon's IoT Network Continuity flyer (PDF created Oct 8, 2026) offers eSIM-based 'automated selection and switching ... between primary and backup solutions or public and private networks', managed in ThingSpace, 'without extra hardware or truck rolls' (https://www.verizon.com/business/resources/flyers/iot-network-continuity.pdf). (3) Verizon's RedCap page promises 'a stable, long-term 15-year operational runway' with LTE fallback (https://www.verizon.com/business/products/internet-of-things/iot-networks/5g-standalone-redcap/). Verizon has 'no imminent plans to turn ... down' NB-IoT (https://www.lightreading.com/iot/at-t-to-discontinue-nb-iot-but-t-mobile-and-verizon-keep-the-faith). (4) Multi-carrier devices lock in a network once: NAPCO's MAX3 'upon power up ... will lock-in the best carrier automatically' (https://www.napcosecurity.com/media/pdfs/A893-StarLink-MaxFire3.pdf). MarketSpark documents sites that showed 'full bars' and still failed because of poor RSRQ (https://www.marketspark.com/understanding-cellular-signal-quality/). (5) Rutgers runs cellular-dependent fleets: blue lights replaced in 2023 that 'send daily health reports' monitored by Knightscope, carrier unverified (https://it.rutgers.edu/rutgers-next-gen-network/next-gen-network-enhancements/), and NAPCO StarLink communicators in 450 buildings (https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/napco-security-technologies-announces-project-at-rutgers-university-301088901.html).
- **Verizon tech:** ThingSpace (Connectivity Management, Device Diagnostics and callbacks APIs), Verizon IoT Network Continuity eSIM profile switching, LTE-M/NB-IoT and Cat-1, 5G SA RedCap with LTE fallback, and ThingSpace self-serve 'Jump in' lines from $1.50 per device per month (https://thingspace.verizon.com/start/).
- **Verizon money:** Verizon Business wireless service revenue (IoT connections). Honest size: small per campus. A few hundred devices at $1.50-10 per month is about $5-30K a year. The value is a repeatable win-back motion for enterprise fleets stranded by T-Mobile's 2G shutdown and LTE-only gating, plus 'Verizon loses here' quality tickets that point network planning at indoor defects. This is the weakest money case of the five.
- **Dated anchor:** T-Mobile's 2G shutdown on Aug 3, 2026; Verizon's IoT Network Continuity flyer dated Oct 8, 2026; T-Mobile's LTE-only activation gating from Jan 1, 2026.
- **Primary data:** (a) A census of Rutgers' cellular IoT by carrier, radio technology and module. Sources: walk audit plus Facilities, Parking, Housing and Dining vendor lists covering EV chargers, pay stations, laundry and vending telemetry, blue lights, bus telematics, BAS gateways and lab sensors, with IMEI TAC lookups. (b) Two weeks of three-network RF logging (RSRP/RSRQ/SINR and attach success on Verizon, AT&T and T-Mobile LTE-M/Cat-1 modules on Raspberry Pis) at about 30 device locations. This includes basements and one SHI home game, and measures how often the best network flips. (c) A count of campus devices that went dark on Aug 3, 2026, from vendor and Facilities tickets.
- **Demo:** VIDEO (2 min): Zoom in on a pay-station modem label reading 'LTE-only, T-Mobile'. A heat map then shows the best network at each device location across 14 nights, with flips highlighted. A Raspberry Pi on a Verizon eSIM switches profile on command from the ThingSpace dashboard. The fleet summary reads: '37 devices; 9 at risk; Verizon best at 21; 6 Verizon-loses tickets'. POSTER TABLE: the three-network logger running live, the ThingSpace dashboard, and a judge-pressable 'kill primary' button that shows continuity failover.
- **Why unique:** It is built on a Verizon product flyer two weeks old and a competitor's Aug 2026 shutdown, both after LLM cutoffs. The data comes from a campus device census only a Rutgers team can get. It is not a consumer app and not a slicing idea.
- **Biggest risk:** (1) The money is small and the carrier is often chosen by the device vendor (ChargePoint, NAPCO), not Rutgers. (2) Rutgers' fleet may be mostly Wi-Fi or wired. (3) Network Continuity's backup may only work across Verizon or private networks, not rival carriers. (4) It sits next to Last Copper's endpoint supervision (communicators, blue lights). (5) The T-Mobile LTE-refarm timeline is a leak.
